  computer" — HOW-TO GAP): top results are six results, none
  is an Alcatel-specific phone-to-USB photo-backup guide.
  Alcatel specifics verified live: the one-tap route exists on
  seven phones only — the A7, Idol 5, Idol 5S, 1X, 3 (2018), 5
  and 7 clear Android 7.0 and list USB OTG; three more clear
  Android 7.0 but their OTG is disputed between sources — the
  5V, 3L (2019) and 3X (2020) — so on those three SnapDisk
  installs but the one-tap route is unproven until a drive
  mounts; three more clear Android 7.0 but no source states
  OTG for them at all — the 1S (2019), 1V (2019) and 3 (2019)
  — so on those three SnapDisk installs but the one-tap route
  is unproven too; two more clear Android 7.0 but list OTG
  No — the 1 and 1SE — so on those two there is no phone route
  at all; six run below Android 7.0 and cannot run SnapDisk at
  all — the A3, Idol 4, Idol 4S, Pixi 4, Pop 4 and U5 all on
  6.0. Three take USB Type-C, so a USB-C drive plugs straight
  into those three; twelve take micro-USB. All twenty-one take
  a microSD card, so the card-reader route works on all
  twenty-one of them, with the ceilings disputed on five.
  Conflicts between sources, all named in the article: the
  Idol 5S's Android is two numbers — 7.0 per gadgets360's
  European listing, 7.1 per ubergizmo, androidheadlines,
  mobilegizmo, kenyaprice and gadgets360's US listing — and
  its microSD ceiling is two numbers — up to 256 GB per
  gadgets360, up to 512 GB per mobileplus and gadgets360's US
  listing — and its battery is two numbers — 2850 mAh per the
  European listing, 2620 mAh per the US listing — and its
  chipset is two numbers — Helio P20 in Europe, Snapdragon 625
  in the US; the Idol 5's microSD ceiling is two numbers — up
  to 128 GB per gadgets360, up to 256 GB per androidheadlines
  and ubergizmo; the A7's RAM is two numbers — 3 GB per
  gadgets360 and androidheadlines, 4 GB per gadgets360's
  first-impressions piece — and its microSD ceiling is two
  numbers — up to 128 GB per gadgets360's A7 page, up to 32 GB
  in the IFA launch article's A7 paragraph; the Alcatel 1's
  Android is two numbers — 8.1 Go edition per UL benchmarks,
  mobilelok and the user manual, 8.0 per smartprix and
  manua.ls — and its cameras are two numbers — a 5 MP rear
  camera and a 2 MP front camera per UL benchmarks, an 8 MP
  rear camera and a 5 MP front camera per smartprix; the 1X's
  battery is two numbers — 3000 mAh per GSMArena's 2019
  listing, 2460 mAh per mobilegizmo's listing, which matches
  the 2018 original; the 3 (2018)'s connector is two numbers
  — microUSB per GSMArena's spec page, USB Type-C per
  GSMArena's MWC 2018 hands-on; the 5's Android is two numbers
  — 8.0 per UL benchmarks, 7.1 per mobilegizmo — and its
  chipset is two numbers — MT6750 per UL benchmarks, MT6750T
  per mobilegizmo; the 5V's OTG is disputed — GSMArena's USB
  row lists only microUSB 2.0, whatmobile's compare lists OTG
  Yes; the 7's connector is two numbers — USB Type-C per
  phonearena, phonesdata and mobilegizmo, microUSB per
  ubergizmo — and its RAM is two numbers — 2 GB per phonearena
  and phonesdata, 2 GB or 3 GB per gadgetsranked; the 1S
  (2019)'s OTG is unstated in the sources found, and its
  refreshes split — the 2020 refresh runs Android 10 and is
  listed as not supporting OTG by manuals.plus, while the 2021
  refresh runs Android 11 with OTG Yes per eIMEI24 and No per
  manuals.plus; the 1V (2019)'s OTG is unstated in the sources
  found, and its 2020 refresh runs Android 10 Go with OTG Yes
  per usesinurdu and No per manuals.plus; the 3 (2019)'s
  Android is two numbers — 8.1 per gadgets360 and eIMEI24, 9.0
  per phonearena, which lists 9.0 Pie and 8.1 Oreo — and its
  storage is two listings — 32 GB per gadgets360 and eIMEI24,
  32 GB or 64 GB per phonearena; the 3L (2019)'s OTG is
  disputed — mobilegizmo lists USB OTG Support Yes,
  manuals.plus lists it as not supporting OTG — and its Android
  is two numbers — 8.1 per mobilegizmo, 9.0 per phonearena,
  which lists 9.0 Pie and 8.1 Oreo; the 3X (2020)'s OTG is
  disputed — smartprix lists USB on-the-go, GSMArena's spec
  listing shows only microUSB 2.0; the Idol 4S's microSD
  ceiling is two numbers — up to 512 GB per gadgets360,
  phonemore and phonebunch, up to 200 GB per notebookcheck; the
  Pop 4's microSD ceiling is three numbers — up to 32 GB per
  androidadvices and gadgetexplained, up to 32 GB or 128 GB per
  eIMEI24, up to 256 GB per phonesdata — and its chipset is two
  numbers — Snapdragon 210 per eIMEI24, Snapdragon 212 per
  androidadvices. Kimovil lists the 1S and the 1S (2021) with
  Google Mobile Services, and the 1S and 3L user manuals list
  Google Play as the app store, so Play is the install route on
  all covered models once the listing is live, and the GitHub
  APK sideload stays the route only until then.
  VaultDrive needs Android 8 or later per its own site, so
  twelve of the fifteen can run it — the 1, 1X, 3 (2018), 5,
  5V, 7, 1S, 1V, 3 (2019), 3L, 3X and 1SE — and AppBrain lists
  12.1 or later, which no covered Alcatel phone clears, since
  the newest covered model runs Android 11. FotoSafe needs
  Android 9 or later with USB host or OTG support, so six of
  the fifteen clear the version floor — the 1S, 1V, 3 (2019),
  3L, 3X and 1SE — but the OTG condition is proven on none of
  the six — the 1SE lists OTG No, the 3L and 3X have OTG
  disputed, and no source states OTG for the 1S, 1V or 3
  (2019) — so FotoSafe is proven on zero of the fifteen until
  a drive mounts. Alcatel has no phone-to-USB backup tool of
  its own — no Alcatel-specific backup advice turned up in any
  source beyond a computer copy, Google Drive, or Google
  Photos; the devicesfaq backup FAQ names the computer copy as
  the most economical method, and names the file manager on an
  Alcatel phone as Files, or Files by Google.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 19, 2026 (WL-143, 111th how-to):
  docs/alcatel-photos-to-usb.html.

  phonesdata do not apply to the 2017 P8, so neither listing is
  used as a source here.
  VaultDrive needs Android 8 or later per its own site, so
  four of the fifteen can run it — the P11, P11 3D, U Pro and
  U2 — and AppBrain lists 12.1 or later, which no covered
  Elephone phone clears, since the newest covered model runs
  Android 9. FotoSafe needs Android 9 or later, so two of the
  fifteen clear it — the P11 3D and U2. Elephone has no
  phone-to-USB backup tool of its own — no Elephone-specific
  backup advice turned up in any source beyond a computer copy,
  Google Drive, or Google Photos; the devicesfaq backup FAQ names
  the computer copy as the most economical method, and names the
  file manager on an Elephone phone as Files, or Files by Google.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.

  those five; every covered Sigma clears Android 7.0, so none
  is blocked by its Android version, unlike Casio; the PQ34
  (Android 7.0) and the PQ37 (Android 8.1) list USB OTG No,
  so they can run SnapDisk but cannot host a drive; OTG is
  unconfirmed on the PQ36 (Android 9.0), PQ38 (Android 10),
  PQ39 Max (Android 9.0), PQ39 Ultra (Android 10) and PQ54
  MAX (Android 10) — no source found states OTG for any of
  them, not even the PQ54 MAX's official manual — so on those
  five the drive mounting is the test; the PQ29 carries an
  8 MP rear camera and a 2 MP front camera, the PQ34, PQ39,
  PQ51 and PQ52 a 13 MP rear camera and a 4.9 MP (PQ51/PQ52:
  5 MP) front camera, the PQ37 a 13 MP rear camera and an
  8 MP front camera, the PQ38 a 13 MP rear camera and a 5 MP
  front camera, the PQ39 Max a 13 MP rear camera and a 5 MP
  front camera, the PQ39 Ultra a 12.98 MP rear camera and a
  1.92 MP front camera, and the PQ54 a 13 MP + 0.3 MP dual
  rear camera and a 5 MP front camera; only the PQ29 and the
  PQ39 Max have a sourced microSD ceiling, both up to 128 GB;
  the PQ51's memory is 2 GB RAM and 16 GB storage per
  devicesfaq and mobilexfiles and 3 GB RAM and 32 GB storage
  per findpare; the PQ37's connector is disputed — findpare
  lists both USB Type-C and micro USB 2.0 for the same phone;
  VaultDrive needs Android 8 or later per its own site, so
  the four Android 7.0 Sigmas can't run it either (AppBrain
  lists 12.1 or later, which no covered Sigma clears); Sigma
  Mobile has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own — the
  only Sigma-specific backup advice found is the hard-reset
  page telling you to export photos to a PC via USB or use
  Google Photos; no source found names the stock file manager
  on a Sigma Mobile phone; no source in this run's searches
  names an X-treme model below the PQ29; the PQ29 is IP69
  and the PQ34, the PQ39 and the PQ54 are IP68.

  tool of its own. Rivals re-verified this run: VaultDrive
  (free up to 200 files or 2 GB, Pro $19.99 once, Android 8+
  per its own site, though AppBrain lists Android 12.1+),
  Photos to USB: OTG Backup (free with ads). Google free
  storage is 5 GB by default since May 2026, up to 15 GB
  with a verified phone number; Storage saver compresses
  photos to 16 MP max, videos to 1080p.

  Blade A75 and OPPO A78 phones — no backup guide, and
  the "75" results are different brands' phones. A fifth
  search (AIM-78) returned the AIM-78S datasheet
  (Android 10 and Android 13, 8 MP front, 13 MP rear,
  microSD up to 2 TB, USB3.1 Type C plus USB 2.0 Type
  A), the AIM-78H datasheet (medical, Android 10), the
  AIM-77S manual (Rockchip RK3568, Android 12, microSD
  up to 1 TB, USB 3.0 Type C, 5 MP front, 8 MP rear)
  and the AIM-68S datasheet (Intel N200/N50, Windows 11
  only) — no backup guide. A sixth search (DeviceOn /
  iService) returned DeviceOn as a device-management
  platform (monitoring, OTA updates, remote desktop,
  backup/recovery of the device itself) — not a
  tablet-to-USB photo copy. A seventh search (rivals)
  re-verified this run: VaultDrive (free up to 200 files
  or 2 GB per backup, Pro $19.99 once, Android 8 or
  later per its own site), Photo Backup Stick DC (free,
  genuine Photo Backup Stick drives only), FotoSafe
  (Android 9 or later with USB host/OTG support, system
  picker, one free run up to 100 media files then
  one-time Pro), PhotoSaver (USB-C devices only).
  Honest limits recorded in the article: no source found
  labels any AIM tablet's USB port OTG — the datasheets
  say only "USB Type-C 3.0" or "USB3.1 Type C" — so on
  all three covered tablets the drive mounting is the
  test, while no tablet lists OTG support; the AIM-78S
  also carries a full-size USB 2.0 Type-A port, but no
  source found says that port hosts a drive; the AIM-75
  runs Android 10 with GMS in its earlier datasheets and
  Android 12 with Android Enterprise Recommended in its
  2024 datasheet, and the AIM-78S runs Android 10 or
  Android 13 by variant (one reseller listing lists
  Android 12 for the AIM-78S-600B20); the AIM-35, the
  AIM-38, the AIM-65 and the AIM-68 run Android 6.0 or
  6.0.1, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, so they
  cannot run SnapDisk at all, and many of those units
  shipped Windows 10 IoT instead of Android, while the
  new AIM-68S is Windows 11 only; no source found states
  whether the AIM-77S or the AIM-78S ship Google Play,
  while the AIM-75 ships Google Mobile Services; the
  AIM-75 carries a 5 MP front and 8 MP rear camera, the
  AIM-77S carries a 5 MP front and 8 MP rear camera, and
  the AIM-78S carries an 8 MP front and 13 MP rear
  camera; the AIM-75 and the AIM-78S take microSD up to
  2 TB and the AIM-77S up to 1 TB; no source found states
  the AIM-77S's screen size or the port position on the
  AIM-77S; no source found states whether any covered
  tablet can charge while the drive is connected (the
  AIM-78S lists a separate 19 VDC-in jack, unconfirmed
  as a mid-backup charging route); "AIM-75" and "AIM-78"
  collide with the CAT S75, the ZTE Blade A75 and the
  OPPO A78, different brands' phones; no source found
  names the stock file manager on an AIM tablet;
  Advantech has no tablet-to-USB photo backup tool of its
  own — DeviceOn/iService is remote device management.
  Fallback for WL-120: RuggON — no RuggON article exists
  in docs/ yet and no searches.md row (rugged Android
  tablets, SOL-PA501 Android 9.0/11 GMS, VIKING II
  Android 14).
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.

  7202, 7212, 7522, 7532, 7622, 7642, 7722, 7742,
  Butterfly) — DECT handsets, not Android smartphones.
  A seventh search (84-series) returned the 8440/8450/
  8452/8453 listings (Camera: No, Memory Card Support:
  No) — Wi-Fi phones, not Android smartphones.
  Honest limits recorded in the article: no source found
  labels any Versity USB port OTG — the datasheets say
  only "USB3.1 Type-C Connector" — so on all four series
  the drive mounting is the test, while no series lists
  OTG support; the 95 and 96 launched on Android 8.1,
  later datasheets list Android 10, and the upgrade to
  Android 13 requires a factory default that wipes the
  device, so back up before upgrading; no upgrade to
  Android 13 is stated for the 92; no source found states
  microSD support or a microSD capacity for any covered
  Versity model, a front-camera spec for the 97, or the

  and the 97 is Google Android Enterprise Recommended,
  and no source found states whether the 92 or the 95
  ship Google Play; the DECT 7000 series and the
  84-series Wi-Fi phones are not Android smartphones and
  cannot run SnapDisk at all; the Versity 90 Series
  turned up by name only; no source found names the
  stock file manager on a Versity phone; Spectralink has
  no phone-to-USB photo backup tool of its own.

  Original; says nothing about i.safe MOBILE or Android);
  cdn.prod.website-files.com, "Transferring photos from your
  iPhone to a USB drive" (iPhone guide — mostly through a
  Windows PC or Mac; says nothing about i.safe MOBILE or
  Android); photocleanse.app, "Transfer Photos from iPhone
  to an External Hard Drive" (iPhone guide — Photos, Export
  Unmodified Original to the drive; says nothing about
  i.safe MOBILE or Android); FotoSafe, "Back up Android
  photos to USB" (the only Android result — a real
  no-computer phone-to-USB guide and a real one-tap app,
  but generic Android, not an i.safe guide: it says nothing
  about an i.safe model, its USB port, or its hazardous-area
  rules). A second search ("i.safe MOBILE IS530 OTG USB
  host flash drive") returned only i.safe operating manuals
  — no guide, no OTG confirmation anywhere.
  IS530.1, IS530.2, IS530.RG, IS530.M1, IS540.1, IS540.2 and
  IS655 covered (i.safe datasheets + operating manuals +
  reseller listings + benchmark listings, Nov 19): IS530.1 —
  Android 10 at launch (OTA to Android 14 announced for
  2025), Snapdragon 660, 4 GB/64 GB, microSD up to 128 GB,
  USB Type-C, replaceable 3600 mAh, 13 MP AF rear + 5 MP
  front, 4.5-inch Gorilla Glass 3 at 854 x 480, 13-pin ISM
  interface; IS530.2 — Zone 2/22 variant of the same M53A01
  hardware, Android 13 in its 2024 datasheet, same specs;
  IS530.RG — non-Ex rugged variant, Android 14 in its March
  2026 datasheet, same specs, magnetic charger port; IS530.M1
  — mining variant, Android 10 at launch, same specs;
  IS540.1 — Android 14 in a reseller listing, Qualcomm
  QCM6490, 8 GB/128 GB, microSD to 128 GB, USB Type-C,
  replaceable 4400 mAh, 48 MP main + 5 MP front, 6-inch full
  HD, 16-pin ISM interface; IS540.2 — Zone 2/22 variant,
  Android 12 in a review, same specs; IS655.RG / IS655.2 —
  Android 10, MediaTek Helio P22, 3 GB/32 GB, microSD up to
  128 GB, micro-USB, 4200 mAh integrated battery, 13 MP AF
  rear + 8 MP front, 5.5-inch HD+ at 720 x 1440.
  Honest limits recorded in the article: no source found
  labels any i.safe MOBILE USB port OTG — every datasheet
  and manual says only that the USB interface is for
  charging and data transfer — so the drive mounting is the
  test on all seven models before buying an adapter; i.safe's
  own manuals say to use the USB interface only outside
  explosion-hazardous areas — wired USB connections are
  prohibited inside them, and only approved accessories may
  be connected — so the backup happens outside the hazardous
  area, with the i.safe PROTECTOR 2.0 cable; the Android
  version differs by source on three models (IS540.1: Android
  12 in a benchmark listing and a review, Android 14 in a
  reseller listing; IS530.2: Android 13 in its 2024 datasheet,
  Android 10 upgradable to 13 in an older reseller sheet;
  IS530.RG: Android 14 in its March 2026 datasheet, Android
  10 upgradable to 13 in an older reseller sheet), so the
  article states no single version for those three; the
  IS540.1's microSD capacity differs by source (up to 128 GB
  in the manuals and one reseller listing, up to 256 GB in
  another), so the article states no capacity for that model;
  the IS655 is the only covered model with micro-USB, so it
  needs a micro-USB OTG adapter, and its battery is
  integrated, not replaceable; Google Play comes with the EEA
  versions only — some variants, such as the mining versions,
  may not have it, so sideload the APK if it is absent; no
  source found names the stock file manager on an i.safe
  device — the manuals point at Google's Android support
  pages — so Method 2 uses the built-in file manager,
  whatever it is called on the unit; no source found states
  the port position on any of the seven models; no IS530.3
  variant turned up in the sources found; FotoSafe needs
  Android 9, so it cannot run on a device still on Android
  7.0 or 8.0 — but every covered i.safe model runs Android
  10 or later, so the floor does not bite here.
  Article published Nov 19, 2026 (WL-114, 82nd how-to):
  docs/isafe-mobile-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-115: Ecom Smart-Ex — no Ecom article
  exists in docs/ yet and no searches.md row (Pepperl+Fuchs
  intrinsically safe Android phones, Smart-Ex 02/03 line).

  (softhandtech.com/can-you-transfer-photos-from-phone-to-memory-stick/
  — transfer with a computer as a middleman, directly with OTG
  support, or through cloud services. Generic Android and
  iPhone, and a manual guide — and it says nothing about a
  Winmate device, and its first method needs a computer).
  Winmate M700DQ8-EX, M700DQ9, M700DM9, M900MGN, P400M9 and
  G900G7 covered (Winmate product pages + reseller listing,
  Nov 18): M700DQ8-EX — Android 9.0, Snapdragon 660, 3 GB/32
  GB, 7-inch 1280x720, 8 MP front + 13 MP rear with LED
  auxiliary light and autofocus, USB Port "1 x USB 3.0 Type C
  OTG", micro SD card slot, IP65, ATEX Zone 2; discontinued,
  phased out and replaced by the M700DQ9; M700DQ9 — Qualcomm
  QCS6490 octa-core up to 2.7 GHz, 8 GB/128 GB, 7-inch
  1280x720, USB Port "1 x USB 3.0 Type C OTG", 5,300 mAh,
  20 hours, IP65, MIL-STD-810H; Android version conflicts
  across Winmate's own page (15.0 spec table, 13 body, 11
  summary line); M700DM9 — Android 11.0, ARM A73 + A53, 4
  GB/64 GB, 7-inch 1280x720, USB Port "1 x USB Type C OTG",
  micro SD card slot, IP65, MIL-STD-810G, 5,300 mAh, 20
  hours; M900MGN — Android 13.0, ARM Genio 510, 4 GB/64 GB,
  8-inch 1280x800, 8 MP front + 13 MP rear with LED auxiliary
  light and autofocus, USB Port "1 x USB 3.0 Type A" and
  "1 x USB 2.0 Type C OTG (support PD 3.0 charging)", micro SD
  card slot, RJ45, IP65, MIL-STD-810H, 4,200 mAh; FAQ says the
  Type-A port is "for connecting standard USB drives";
  P400M9 — Android 11.0, ARM A73 + A53, 4 GB/64 GB, 4-inch
  800x480, 8 MP front + 13 MP rear with LED auxiliary light
  and autofocus, USB Port "1 x USB Type C OTG (support PD 2.0
  charging)", micro SD card slot, IP65, MIL-STD-810H, 2,700
  mAh, 8 hours; screen 4.0 inches in its spec table and 4.3
  inches in its FAQ, battery 2,700 mAh in its spec table and
  "typically around 3000-4000mAh" in its FAQ; G900G7 — 8-inch
  rugged handheld robotic controller, Android 13 (Ubuntu
  Linux 22.04 optional), ARM Genio 510, 4 GB/64 GB, USB Port
  "1 x USB 3.0 (Type-A)" and "1 x USB 2.0 (Type C,PD)",
  IP65, MIL-STD-810H, 3,500 mAh, 5.5 hours (7.5 hours
  high-capacity). All six clear SnapDisk's Android 7.0
  minimum; only the M900MGN and the G900G7 take a standard
  USB-A drive with no adapter — the other four need a USB-C
  OTG adapter, and the G900G7's Type-C port is not labelled
  OTG in either source, so the Type-A port is the confirmed
  host route on that model.
  Honest limits recorded in the article: OTG is confirmed in
  the spec tables on five of the six models, host only
  through the Type-A port on the G900G7; the G900AD runs
  Windows (Intel Core i5) and cannot run SnapDisk at all;
  two Android versions differ by source (M700DQ9 15.0 vs 13
  vs 11 on one Winmate page; P400M9 screen 4.0in vs 4.3in and
  battery 2,700 mAh vs 3000–4000 mAh across its spec table
  and FAQ — all covered models clear the minimum either
  way); no source found states a camera spec for the
  M700DQ9, the M700DM9 or the G900G7, or a microSD capacity
  for any of the six models, or names the stock file manager
  on a Winmate device, or states the port position on any of
  the six; three Winmate models turned up but are not covered
  (M700DM8 by name only in accessory listings, G500G7's
  Android version and OTG support not sourced, FM10MG-V a
  vehicle-mount terminal out of scope); SOTI MobiControl is
  device management, not a photo backup; no other Winmate
  rugged handheld or tablet model turned up in the sources
  found.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-110, 78th how-to):
  docs/winmate-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-111: Juniper Systems — no Juniper article
  exists in docs/ yet and no searches.md row (Mesa/Archer
  rugged Android handhelds/tablets).

  Facts verified from code on fresh main: SAF tree picker

  Search evidence (Nov 18, 2026 live web search): Query 76
  ("back up Handheld photos to USB drive without computer")
  is a real gap — no Handheld-specific phone-to-USB guide
  in the top results at all; not one result even mentions
  Handheld. pixoneye.com, "How to Transfer Photos from Your
  Phone to a USB Stick"
  (pixoneye.com/how-to-transfer-photos-from-phone-to-usb-stick/
  — copy local originals to compatible USB storage with a
  supported file manager on Android or Photos and Files on
  iPhone, make a trial copy first, use Copy rather than Move.
  Generic Android and iPhone, and a manual guide — and it
  says nothing about a Handheld handheld);
  thegadgetians.com, "How to Transfer Files From Phone to USB
  Without Computer: Ultimate Guide"
  (thegadgetians.com/how-to-transfer-files-from-phone-to-usb-without-computer/
  — connect a USB drive to an Android phone with an OTG
  adapter or to an iPhone with a Lightning-to-USB adapter,
  open the file manager, select and copy files, then paste
  onto the USB drive. Generic Android and iPhone, and a manual
  guide — and it says nothing about a Handheld handheld);
  YouTube, "How to Connect USB Drive to Android Phone to Move
  Photos & Videos (Best Method)"
  (youtube.com/watch?v=PYcbV25CczI — OTG adapter + the
  phone's built-in file manager, no computer or internet.
  Generic Android, and a manual guide — and it says nothing
  about a Handheld handheld); jesave.com, "How to Transfer
  Photos from a Phone to a USB Flash Drive"
  (jesave.com/how-to-transfer-photos-from-a-phone-to-a-usb-flash-drive.html
  — plug a USB-C flash drive straight into a USB-C phone, or
  use a dual USB-C + USB-A drive, or a USB adapter for a
  USB-A drive; or use a computer as a middleman. Generic
  Android and iPhone, and a manual guide — and it says nothing
  about a Handheld handheld, and its computer method needs a
  PC in between); dzianisv.github.io, "Back up CipherLab
  photos to a USB drive"
  (dzianisv.github.io/photo-backup/cipherlab-photos-to-usb.html
  — our own earlier SnapDisk article, for CipherLab's RS/RK
  handhelds, not Handheld's Nautiz line — and it says nothing
  about a Handheld handheld, which is why it ranks here: the
  query is so thin that a sibling article for another rugged
  brand is the closest brand-specific result).
  Handheld Nautiz models covered (datasheet/guide/review
  sources, Nov 18): refreshed X2 — Android 9.0 GMS in its
  v1.8 datasheet (Helio P22, 4 GB/64 GB, 4.7-inch, Ports
  "Micro USB 2.0, OTG", microSDXC to 256 GB); X6 — Android
  8.0 Oreo GMS in its v1.0 datasheet (Android 8.1 in Rugged
  PC Review's lineup note), Snapdragon 626, 4 GB/32 GB,
  Ports "Type-C, Qualcomm quick charge 3.0, OTG", microSDXC
  to 128 GB; X9 — Android 7.0 Nougat GMS in its v1.0
  datasheet (Android 11 in a later Handheld press release),
  1.3 GHz quad-core Cortex-A53, 2 GB/16 GB, 5-inch 1280x720,
  13 MP rear camera with autofocus and flash + 2 MP front,
  Connections "Micro USB 2.0, High Speed / OTG", 4,800 mAh,
  microSDHC to 32 GB, IP67; X41 — Android 9.0 GMS (Helio
  P22, 4 GB/64 GB, 4-inch, Ports "Type-C, OTG", 4,400 mAh
  field-replaceable battery, microSDXC to 128 GB, IP65,
  MIL-STD-810G); X81 — Android 12 GMS, 4.8-inch
  sunlight-readable display with full numeric keypad, 13 MP
  rear autofocus camera with LED flash + 5 MP front, IP67,
  5G, -30 to 60 °C, USB A host port on the bottom panel per
  its Quick Start Guide. All five clear SnapDisk's Android
  7.0 minimum; only the X81 takes a standard USB-A drive
  with no adapter — the X2 and the X9 need a micro-USB OTG
  adapter and the X6 and the X41 need a USB-C OTG adapter.
  Cannot run SnapDisk: original X2 (Android 6.0, no OTG
  listed in its v1.2 datasheet), X4 (Android 4.2 or Windows
  Embedded Handheld 6.5; USB host only for the Ethernet
  cradle), X8 (Android 4.2.2 or Windows Embedded Handheld
  6.5.3; USB A Host listed in its manual, but the OS
  excludes it).
  Honest limits recorded in the article: OTG or host is
  confirmed only for the five covered models — the original
  X2's datasheet lists no OTG and the X4's USB host exists
  only for the Ethernet cradle; three older models cannot
  run SnapDisk at all (original X2 Android 6.0, X4 Android
  4.2/Windows, X8 Android 4.2.2/Windows); three Android
  versions differ by source (X2 6.0 vs 9.0 GMS across its
  two datasheet generations, X6 8.0 vs 8.1, X9 7.0 vs 11 —
  all covered models clear the minimum either way); no
  source found names the stock file manager on a Nautiz
  handheld; no source found states a camera spec for the
  X41, the X6 or the refreshed X2; no source found states a
  microSD capacity for the X81; no source found states the
  port position on the X2, the X6 or the X9 (X41 USB-C is
  on the left side, X81 USB A host is on the bottom panel);
  the Nautiz X1 turned up by name only — no Android version
  or OTG support sourced; the MaxGo suite is device
  management, not a photo backup; no other Nautiz model
  turned up in the sources found.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-109, 77th how-to):
  docs/handheld-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-110: Winmate — no Winmate article exists
  in docs/ yet and no searches.md row (M-series rugged
  enterprise Android handhelds/tablets).
  The Getac ZX-series fallback was considered and rejected:
  getac-photos-to-usb.html already covers the ZX70, ZX80
  and ZX10, so it is not an uncovered brand.

  other M3 Mobile handheld model turned up in the sources

  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-108, 76th how-to):
  docs/m3mobile-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-109: Handheld (Nautiz) — no Handheld or
  Nautiz article exists in docs/ yet and no searches.md row

  (trucoteca.com/en/how-to-download-photos-from-your-mobile-phone-to-the-device/
  — connect a USB drive with an OTG adapter and move photos

  No Computer" (coticsy.com/vaultdrive.html — plug a flash drive

  (cellularnews.com/device-reviews-and-comparisons/smartphones/how-to-move-pictures-to-a-flash-drive-from-google-phone/
  — connect a USB storage device using an OTG adapter and copy

  "Transfer Photos from Phone to Memory Stick: iPhone and

  (itechguides.com/transfer-photos-from-phone-to-memory-stick-a-comprehensive-guide/
  — match the drive to the phone's connector, copy a test batch,
  verify, delete originals only afterward. Generic Android and
  iPhone, and a manual guide — and it warns that file-manager
  menus and USB-host support vary by manufacturer and model,
  which is exactly the Point Mobile question); Photos to USB:
  OTG Backup
  (play.google.com/store/apps/details?id=com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb
  — one tap copies the whole gallery to a pen drive, only new
  photos next time; no internet, no cloud, no account. A rival
  app, not a guide — and free with ads, a one-time purchase
  removing the ads).
  Point Mobile models covered (spec/manual/retailer sources, Nov
  18): PM30 — Android 10, "Standard USB2.0 OTG"; PM30hc
  healthcare variant — Android 10 (upgradeable to Android 14),
  "Standard USB2.0 OTG"; PM45 — shipped Android 7, upgrade to
  Android 8 (UL benchmarks lists Android 8.1), "Standard USB2.0
  OTG"; PM67 — Android 11 (upgradable to Android 13); PM75 —
  Android 11 (upgradable to Android 14), "USB 2.0 Type C OTG";
  PM85 — Android 8.1 (upgradable to Android 9); PM86 — Android 11
  or 13 (upgradable to Android 14); PM90 — Android 9 (a later

  speed USB 3.0 Initiator and Target"; PM95 — Android 13
  (upgradable to Android 18); PM451 — Android 9 (upgradable to
  Android 11). Below the Android 7.0 minimum: PM80 (Android 5.0),

  route there; PM450 runs Windows Embedded, not Android.

  confirmed in the spec listings on only five of the ten
  handhelds — the PM30, the PM30hc, the PM45, the PM75 and the
  PM90 — on the other five (PM67, PM85, PM86, PM95, PM451) the

  (Point Mobile datasheet lists USB-C, UL benchmarks lists
  microUSB 2.0); no source found states the connector type on
  the PM95 or the PM451 at all; the PM90's Android version
  differs by datasheet (9 vs 11); the PM45's Android version
  differs by source (7→8 upgrade table vs 8.1) — either way both
  clear the minimum.

  the RK25 — no source found confirms OTG or host support on the
  RK26 at all, so the drive mounting is the test on that model;
  the RS36's Android version differs by source (12 or 14), either
  way above the minimum; no other RS/RK model turned up in the
  sources found, so the article covers only the RS36, the RS38,
  the RK26, and the RK25.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-100, 69th how-to):
  docs/cipherlab-photos-to-usb.html.

  Pixoneye, "How to Transfer Photos from Your Phone to a USB

  — connect a USB drive with an OTG adapter, open Files by Google,
  find DCIM/Camera, select a small batch, and Copy to the drive.
  Generic Android, and a manual file-manager guide — and it warns
  that a connector fitting physically does not prove the phone can
  read or write the drive, and that not every phone supports USB
  host at all); AskFilo, "how to transfer pictures from an android
  phone to a flashdrive"
  (askfilo.com/user-question-answers-smart-solutions/how-to-transfer-pictures-from-an-android-phone-to-a-3436303837303432
  — connect a USB drive with an OTG adapter, open the file
  manager, find DCIM/Camera, and Copy to the drive — or use a PC
  as a middleman. Generic Android, and a manual file-manager guide
  — and its computer route is the only one that works if the
  device does not host drives at all); Dr.Fone, "4 Practical Ways
  to Transfer Photos from Android to USB Flash Drive"
  (drfone.wondershare.com/android-transfer/how-to-transfer-photos-from-android-to-usb.html
  — connect a USB drive with an OTG adapter and copy with the file
  manager, use a PC as a middleman via USB File Transfer mode, or
  go wireless through Google Drive or AirDroid. Generic Android,
  and a manual guide — and its bulk-export route is Dr.Fone's own
  Windows and Mac software, which needs a computer in between);
  connect.gmb.org.uk, "How to Transfer Pictures from Android to
  Flash Drive A Simple Guide"
  (connect.gmb.org.uk/how-to-transfer-pictures-from-android-to-flash-drive/
  — connect the flash drive with a USB OTG cable or straight into
  a USB-C port, then copy pictures with the file manager — or use
  a computer as a middleman. Generic Android, and a manual guide —
  and it notes that older devices may need an OTG adapter at all);
  C66 datasheet
  (osiris.co.za/web/content/17830?unique=8b4a9be39e9136b9739bcf957c16f6f7ae1bd69a&download=true
  — the C66: Android 11 / 13, 5.5-inch display, 13 MP camera,
  with interfaces listed as "USB Type-C, USB 3.1, OTG". A
  datasheet, not a guide — and the confirmation that the C66
  lists OTG in its interfaces and runs Android 11 or 13, so it
  clears SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum); Chainway C66 UHF
  product page (chainway.net/Products/Info/75 — the C66: Android
  13 / 11, Qualcomm octa-core processor, 5.5-inch display, 13 MP
  camera, with interfaces listed as "USB Type-C, USB 3.1, OTG".
  A product page, not a guide — and the confirmation, from
  Chainway's own site, that the C66 lists OTG and runs Android 11
  or 13); C66 user manual
  (manuals.plus/chainway/c66-android-mobile-computer-barcode-scanner-manual.pdf
  — the C66: Android 7.1 / Android 9.0 in this manual edition,
  with a section "6.7 OTG Function" that describes a cradle OTG
  connection: install the device onto the cradle and select OTG
  mode on the pop-up menu to switch on OTG. A manual, not a guide
  — and the confirmation that the only Chainway OTG instructions
  found describe the cradle, not a direct adapter — and that
  older C66 units run Android 7.1 or 9.0, which still clears
  SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum); C72 user manual
  (manuals.plus/m/de811c9f0650bc024acc8c88aff45e8c737fe7cf8a33d1bd54d9bb6b38ac33a5
  — the C72: Android 6.0, 5.2-inch display, 13 MP camera, with
  interfaces listed as "USB2.0, Type-C, OTG". A manual, not a
  guide — and the confirmation that the original C72 runs Android
  6.0, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, so it cannot run
  SnapDisk at all — while still listing OTG in its interfaces);
  C72 datasheet (fqingenieria.com/img/chainway/C72-EN.pdf — the
  C72: Android 8.1 and Android 11.0 variants, 5.2-inch display,
  13 MP camera, with interfaces listed as "USB 2.0 Type-C, OTG".
  A datasheet, not a guide — and the confirmation that current
  C72 units run Android 8.1 or 11 with OTG, so they clear
  SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, unlike the original Android 6.0
  unit); C61 spec page
  (raptool.com/devices/chainway-c61-mobile-computer-android-11-13/
  — the C61: Android 11 / 13, Qualcomm octa-core processor,
  keypad, 6,700 mAh battery, with interfaces listed as "USB
  Type-C, USB 3.1, OTG". A spec page, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the C61 lists OTG and runs Android 11 or 13,
  so it clears SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum).
  Honest limits recorded in the article: USB OTG is confirmed in
  the spec listings on all three models — the C66, the C72, and
  the C61 all list "OTG" in their interfaces — but the C66
  manual's OTG section describes a cradle connection, so if a
  direct OTG adapter doesn't mount the drive, the cradle is the
  documented route; the original C72 cannot run SnapDisk at all —
  it runs Android 6.0, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum —
  while current C72 units run Android 8.1 or 11, the C66 runs
  Android 11 or 13 (older units Android 7.1 or 9.0), and the C61
  runs Android 11 or 13, so every current model clears the
  minimum; no other C-series model turned up in the sources
  found, so the article covers only the C66, the C72, and the
  C61.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-99, 68th how-to):
  docs/chainway-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-100: CipherLab — no CipherLab article exists in
  docs/ yet (RS/RK-series enterprise Android handhelds).

  (getac.com/content/dam/getac/product-spec-data-pdf/us/Getac_ZX80_US_Product.pdf
  — the ZX80: Android 15, Snapdragon QCS6490, 12 GB RAM and
  256 GB storage, with I/O listed as a single "USB 3.2 Gen 1
  Type-C (with DisplayPort & Power Delivery)" port plus MicroSD
  and a docking connector. A spec sheet, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the ZX80 runs Android 15 in Getac's current
  spec, with a USB-C port and no host or OTG mention anywhere in
  it); Getac ZX80 tech specs
  (thirdwaverugged.com/zx80-tech-specs/ — the ZX80: Android 15,
  Snapdragon QCS6490, 12 GB RAM, 256 GB storage, with I/O listed
  as a USB-C port, MicroSD, and a docking connector. A spec page,
  not a guide — and the confirmation that the ZX80's current spec
  is Android 15, while older datasheets list Android 13, so either
  way it clears SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, with a USB-C port
  and no host or OTG mention anywhere in it); Getac ZX70 user
  manual (usermanuals.au/getac/zx70/manual?p=24 — connect the
  device to a computer with the USB cable to transfer files, under
  "Connecting to a Computer via USB". A manual, not a guide — and
  the confirmation that Getac's own file advice for the ZX70 is a
  connection to a host computer, with no USB-drive route at all);
  Getac ZX70 specifications (usermanuals.au/getac/zx70/manual?p=55
  — the ZX70: Android 6.0, 7-inch display, 32 GB storage, with
  ports listed as one USB 2.0 port and one Micro-USB 2.0 port. A
  spec page, not a guide — and the confirmation that the ZX70 runs
  Android 6.0, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, so it cannot
  run SnapDisk at all).
  Honest limits recorded in the article: USB host support is
  confirmed in the sources found only on the ZX10 line — the ZX10
  and the ZX10-Ex G2 spec listings give a dedicated "USB 2.0
  (Host)" port, so a regular USB-A flash drive plugs straight into
  them with no adapter at all — while no source found mentions
  host or OTG support on the ZX80 at all, so the drive mounting is
  the test; the ZX70 cannot run SnapDisk at all — it runs Android
  6.0, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum — while the ZX80 runs
  Android 13 (Getac's current spec lists Android 15) and the ZX10
  runs Android 11, and the ZX10-Ex G2 runs Android 15 (some older
  listings say Android 13), so the minimum is not the blocker on
  the newer models, the host support is; no ZX50 or ZX90 model
  turned up in the sources found, so the article covers only the
  ZX70, the ZX80, and the ZX10.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-98, 67th how-to):
  docs/getac-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-99: Chainway — no Chainway article exists in
  docs/ yet (C-series enterprise Android handhelds).

  mention anywhere in it); Urovo DT66 datasheet
  (acventis.com/media/e8/b2/99/1751374707/ACVENTIS_urovo_dt66_datasheet.pdf
  — the DT66 runs Android 13.0, GMS/AER, upgradable to Android 18,
  octa-core 2.4 GHz, with interfaces listed as "USB Type-C,
  Pogo-pin" and a microSD card up to 256 GB. A datasheet, not a
  guide — and the confirmation that the DT66 runs Android 13, so
  it clears SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, with a USB Type-C port
  and no host or OTG mention anywhere in it); Urovo DT50 5G
  instruction manual (manuals.plus/ae/1005008655164534.pdf — the
  DT50 5G runs Android 10, with a 16 MP rear camera, and it "does
  not support Google Mobile Services (GMS)". A manual, not a guide
  — and the confirmation that the DT50 5G runs Android 10, so it
  clears SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, plus the warning that its
  Play Store route is closed: install the APK from GitHub, not
  from Play); Cleverence, "How to Connect the Urovo DT50 to a PC"
  (cleverence.com/articles/technical-manuals/how-to-connect-the-urovo-dt50-to-a-pc/
  — connect the DT50 to a PC with a USB-C cable, tap the USB
  notification, choose File Transfer (MTP), and drag and drop
  files in File Explorer. A PC guide, not a phone-to-USB guide —
  and the confirmation that every Urovo-specific transfer guide
  found is a computer guide, with no USB-drive route at all).
  Honest limits recorded in the article: USB host or OTG support
  is confirmed in the sources found only on the DT50 — its spec
  listings give the data interface as "USB Type-C(OTG)", and the
  DT50U/DT50P user guide describes a host mode — while no source
  found mentions host or OTG support on the DT40 or the DT66 at
  all, so the drive mounting is the test; every DT model listed
  clears SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum — the DT40 runs Android
  9.0, the DT40S runs Android 12, the DT50 runs Android 9.0, 11,
  or 13 depending on the variant, the DT50 5G runs Android 10, and
  the DT66 runs Android 13, upgradable to Android 18 — so the
  minimum is not the blocker on this line, the host support is;
  no DT30 or DT60 model turned up in the sources found, so the
  article covers only the DT40, the DT50, and the DT66; the DT50
  5G ships without Google Mobile Services, so the Play Store is not
  on it — install the APK from GitHub, not from Play; Urovo has no
  phone-to-USB backup tool of its own — the only Urovo-specific
  backup advice found is the DT50 manuals' USB connection to a host
  computer, with no USB-drive route at all.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-97, 66th how-to):
  docs/urovo-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-98: Getac — no Getac article exists in
  docs/ yet (ZX-series rugged Android tablets/handhelds).

  generic manual guides — most of them about camera memory cards,
  not the Toughbook's own gallery. PetaPixel, "No Laptop? No
  Problem: How to Back Up Photos in the Field With Your
  Smartphone" (petapixel.com/2025/05/11/no-laptop-no-problem-how-to-back-up-photos-in-the-field-with-your-smartphone/
  — a phone plus a USB hub plus a card reader plus an SSD, copying
  a camera's SD or CFexpress card with the phone's file manager.
  Generic, and a manual guide — and about a camera's memory card,
  not the Toughbook's own gallery); Luminous Landscape forum,
  "Transferring files from camera to hard drive without laptop"
  (forum.luminous-landscape.com/index.php?topic=129861.0%3Bwap2 —
  wireless hard drives with an SD slot that copy a card
  automatically, such as the WD My Passport Wireless Pro. A forum
  thread, not a guide — and about camera cards and a dedicated
  drive); desnoulez.fr, "How to back up camera photos or SD Card
  without a laptop"
  (desnoulez.fr/en/how-to-back-up-camera-photos-while-traveling-no-laptop-needed/
  — the same phone-as-bridge idea, copying a camera's card with
  the file manager. Generic, and a manual guide — and again about
  a camera's card); devicesfaq, "Frequently asked questions
  Panasonic Toughbook P-01K"
  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/panasonic-toughbook-p-01k
  — the only Toughbook-specific result: its backup answer is to
  connect the P-01K to a computer with a USB cable and copy the
  files with the file browser, or use Google Drive (15 GB free) or
  Google Photos. A FAQ page, not a guide — and the confirmation
  that the P-01K runs Android 8.1 with 16 GB of internal memory
  and a micro-USB charging port, with no USB-drive route offered at
  all); Photos to USB: OTG Backup
  (play.google.com/store/apps/details?id=com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb
  — plug a pen drive into your phone, one tap copies every photo
  and video to the drive, no internet, no cloud, no account; free
  with ads, a one-time purchase removes the ads. The one-tap app
  alternative, generic Android, not a Toughbook guide);
  thegadgetians, "How to Transfer Files From Phone to USB Without
  Computer"
  (thegadgetians.com/how-to-transfer-files-from-phone-to-usb-without-computer/
  — connect a USB drive with an OTG adapter, open the file
  manager, select and copy files, then paste onto the USB drive.
  Generic Android, and a manual file-manager guide).
  Honest limits recorded in the article: the Toughbook line is
  mostly Windows laptops, so the article covers only the Android
  handhelds and tablets (FZ-N1, FZ-T1, FZ-S1, FZ-A3, FZ-L1,
  P-01K); the original FZ-N1 runs Android 6.0.1 (Panasonic Wiki),
  below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, so it cannot run SnapDisk
  at all — only the FZ-N1 mk2, on Android 8.1 (Panasonic spec
  sheet), can; USB host support is listed only on the FZ-N1 mk2
  and the FZ-L1 (Panasonic's own spec sheets list their micro-USB
  port as "option to be host"), while host or OTG support is
  unconfirmed in the sources found on the FZ-T1 (Android 8.1,
  MobileGizmo), the FZ-S1 (Android 10, USB-C, Notebookcheck), the
  FZ-A3 (Android 9.0, USB-C, UL benchmarks), and the P-01K
  (Android 8.1, micro-USB, devicesfaq) — the drive mounting is the
  test on those models; the FZ-S1's microSD support is unconfirmed
  in the sources found, so the card is not leaned on for that
  model; most search results are about camera memory cards, with
  "Panasonic" meaning the camera brand, not the device — the
  article is about the photos stored on the Toughbook itself; the
  adapter differs by model (USB-C OTG on the FZ-S1/FZ-A3,
  micro-USB OTG on the FZ-N1 mk2/FZ-T1/FZ-L1/P-01K); Windows
  Toughbooks are out of scope entirely.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-92, 61st how-to):
  docs/panasonic-toughbook-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-93: Zebra — no Zebra article exists in docs/
  yet (TC-series enterprise Android handhelds).

## WL-91 — Back up Evolveo photos to USB article, 60th how-to (DONE)

- Owner: SEO/GEO lane lead. State: DONE.
  New docs/evolveo-photos-to-usb.html ("Back up Evolveo photos to a
  USB drive (no computer)"), following the WL-54–WL-90 article
  template exactly (navy/cyan, TL;DR first, no JS, text-only, reuses
  docs/screenshots-01-settings-defaults.png as the lead shot).
  Blog index (JSON-LD + post block), sitemap.xml, llms.txt,
  llms-full.txt, feed.xml updated. Title 48 chars, meta description
  157 chars, JSON-LD validated by parse; sitemap and feed XML parse.
  Facts verified from code on fresh main: SAF tree picker
  (MainActivity.kt OpenDocumentTree +
  takePersistableUriPermission); oldest-first via MediaStore
  DATE_TAKEN ascending (PhotoQuery.kt, DATE_TAKEN else
  DATE_MODIFIED fallback); free 5 GB per run
  (ProPrefs.FREE_BACKUP_BYTES_LIMIT); Pro $9.99 one-time =
  auto-backup on connect + unlimited size (strings.xml
  pro_price, pro_unlocked, limit_reached_detail); no INTERNET
  permission in AndroidManifest.xml; FAT32 files >=4 GiB skipped
  with reason (FilesystemInfo.FAT32_MAX_FILE_BYTES); raw
  byte-stream copy (BackupEngine.copyWithResume — no
  decode/re-encode) with size verification after copy.
  Search evidence (Nov 18, 2026 live web search): Query 59 ("back
  up Evolveo photos to USB drive without computer") is a real gap —
  no Evolveo-specific phone-to-USB guide in the top 5 results at
  all; the Evolveo-specific results are spec pages and manuals that
  give no USB-drive advice, and the rest are generic manual guides.
  YouTube, "How to Connect USB Drive to Android Phone to Move
  Photos & Videos (Best Method)"
  (youtube.com/watch?v=PYcbV25CczI — OTG connection and the phone's
  built-in file manager, no computer or internet needed. Generic
  Android, and a manual file-manager guide); VaultDrive
  (coticsy.com/vaultdrive.html — plug a flash drive or SSD into your
  phone, choose what to back up, tap Start; free for up to 200 files
  or 2 GB per backup, Pro $19.99 once; needs Android 8 or later. The
  one-tap app alternative, generic Android, not an Evolveo guide);
  softhandtech, "Transfer Photos from Phone to Memory Stick"
  (softhandtech.com/can-you-transfer-photos-from-phone-to-memory-stick/
  — a computer as middleman, a direct OTG transfer, or cloud
  services. Generic Android, and a manual guide); thegadgetians,
  "How to Transfer Files From Phone to USB Without Computer"
  (thegadgetians.com/how-to-transfer-files-from-phone-to-usb-without-computer/
  — connect a USB drive with an OTG adapter, open the file manager,
  select and copy files, then paste onto the USB drive. Generic
  Android, and a manual file-manager guide); Substack, "saving
  Photos for necessary updates"
  (curiousbluenoser.substack.com/p/saving-photos-for-necessary-updates
  — plug a picture-keeper flash drive into the phone's charging port,
  open the drive's app, and tap Start. Generic, built around a
  specific drive's own app).
  Honest limits recorded in the article: the G9 runs Android 10 with
  USB OTG listed (mobilespecs) and the G5 runs Android 9.0 with USB-C
  (manual) but OTG is unconfirmed on the G5; the G4 lists OTG
  (FindPare, which says its information is "not ascertained") but its
  Android version is unconfirmed in the sources found; the Q8 LTE's
  OTG is disputed (Alza's description lists "microUSB OTG", FindPare
  lists USB OTG as No) and it runs Android 4.4; the Q5 runs Android
  5.1 — both below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, so neither can run
  it; the Z4, the X5, and the Z6 are feature phones, not Android
  phones, so they are out of scope; the adapter differs by model
  (USB-C OTG on the G5/G9, micro-USB OTG on the G4/Q5/Q8).
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-91, 60th how-to):
  docs/evolveo-photos-to-usb.html.
  Fallback for WL-92: Panasonic Toughbook — no Panasonic article
  exists in docs/ yet.

  Generic Android, and a manual file-manager guide); YouTube,
  "How to Transfer Photos, Videos and any Files from Android
  Phone to USB Pendrive (USB Drive Tutorial)"
  (youtube.com/watch?v=ch1evyu5PPM — verify OTG compatibility
  first, connect the drive via an OTG adapter, open the file
  manager, and copy or move. Generic Android, and a manual
  file-manager guide); thegadgetians, "How to Transfer Files
  From Phone to USB Without Computer: Ultimate Guide"
  (thegadgetians.com/how-to-transfer-files-from-phone-to-usb-without-computer/
  — connect a USB drive with an OTG adapter, open the file
  manager, select and copy files, then paste onto the USB drive.
  Generic Android, and a manual file-manager guide — and it
  notes that not all Android devices support OTG); softhandtech,
  "Transfer Photos from Phone to Memory Stick: A Comprehensive
  Guide"
  (softhandtech.com/can-you-transfer-photos-from-phone-to-memory-stick/
  — a computer as middleman, a direct OTG transfer, or cloud
  services. Generic Android, and a manual guide — the first and
  third routes need a computer in between or the cloud);
  Android Central forum, "Moving photos to usb"
  (forums.androidcentral.com/threads/moving-photos-to-usb.973408/
  — use the OTG adapter to plug a USB drive in and move files
  in the file manager. Generic Android, and a manual
  file-manager guide); askfilo,
  "how to transfer pictures from an android phone to a
  flashdrive"
  (askfilo.com/user-question-answers-smart-solutions/how-to-transfer-pictures-from-an-android-phone-to-a-3436303837303432
  — plug the flash drive into an OTG adapter, open the file
  manager, find the DCIM folder, and copy to the USB drive.
  Generic Android, and a manual file-manager guide);
  epictechtips, "How to Transfer Pictures from Android Phone to
  USB Flash Drive"
  (epictechtips.com/transfer-pictures-from-android-phone-to-usb-flash-drive/
  — plug the flash drive in, tap the USB notification and choose
  "Transfer photos", then use the file manager — or use a PC as
  an intermediary. Generic Android, and a manual file-manager
  guide); Coolmuster, "How to Transfer Photos from Android to
  Flash Drive [5 Ways]"
  (coolmuster.com/android/transfer-photos-from-android-to-usb-flash-drive.html
  — drag and drop through a computer, or an OTG adapter with the
  file manager. Generic Android, and a manual guide — the first
  route needs a PC in between, and it notes that not all Android
  devices support OTG); JCB ToughPhone TP802 User Manual,
  manualmachine.com
  (manualmachine.com/jcb/toughphonetp802/21089801-user-manual/
  — the manual for the old TP802 feature phone: connect the
  phone to a PC with the USB cable, select "Mass Storage", and
  copy files to the PC; the T-Flash card is formatted via a PC
  first. A manual, not a backup guide — and the confirmation
  that JCB's own file advice is a computer copy, never a USB
  drive); JCB Toughphone and Toughphone Max announced, GSMArena
  (m.gsmarena.com/jcb_toughphone_and_toughphone_max_announced-news-58927.php
  — the Toughphone boots Android 13 and the Toughphone Max
  ships with Android 12; Helio P22 with 6 GB of RAM and 128 GB
  of storage on the Toughphone, Dimensity 700 with 8 GB and
  256 GB on the Max; 4,050 mAh against 12,200 mAh; £300 and
  £600. A launch story, not a guide — and the confirmation that
  both 2023 models run Android 12 or later); JCB Toughphone,
  cashify.in
  (cashify.in/jcb-toughphone-price-in-india — Android 13,
  MediaTek Helio P22, 6 GB / 128 GB, expandable storage up to
  1 TB, "OTG Support, Yes", USB Type-C. A spec page, not a
  guide — and the confirmation that the Toughphone lists OTG
  support and runs Android 13); JCB Toughphone MAX, cashify.in
  (cashify.in/jcb-toughphone-max-price-in-india — Android 12,
  MediaTek Dimensity 700, 8 GB / 256 GB, expandable storage up
  to 1 TB, "OTG Support, Yes", USB Type-C, 12,200 mAh. A spec
  page, not a guide — and the confirmation that the Toughphone
  Max lists OTG support and runs Android 12); JCB Toughphone
  E10 rugged phone review, europesays.com
  (europesays.com/ie/513477/ — a ruggedised Android 15 phone
  per the review text, with a MediaTek Helio G36, 4 GB of RAM,
  64 GB of storage, a microSD card up to 512 GB, and "USB-C
  2.0 (OTG)". A review, not a guide — and the confirmation
  that the E10 lists OTG support; its own spec table says
  Android 16 and its text says Android 15, so the Android
  version is taken as 15 from JCB's own site instead); JCB
  Toughphone E10, Clove
  (clove.co.uk/collections/smartphones-jcb/products/jcb-toughphone-e10?variant=43070213423147
  — Android 15, a 6,500 mAh battery, dual SIM + eSIM, microSD
  up to 512 GB, USB Type-C, IP69K / MIL-STD-810H. A retailer
  page, not a guide — and a second source for the E10's
  Android 15, against the review's spec table saying 16); JCB
  Toughphone E50 5G Rugged Smartphone, rugged.co.uk
  (rugged.co.uk/jcb-toughphone-e50/ — Android 16, 6 GB of RAM
  expandable to 14 GB with virtual RAM, 128 GB of storage
  extendable to 1 TB via microSD, IP68 and IP69K. A retailer
  page, not a guide — and the confirmation that the E50 runs
  Android 16, with no OTG mention); JCB Toughphone T10 Rugged
  Tablet, jcbphone.com
  (jcbphone.com/products/jcb-toughphone-t10 — JCB's own page:
  Android 16, 128 GB of storage expandable to 2 TB via
  microSD, and "18W wired, USB-C with OTG support". A product
  page, not a guide — and the confirmation, from JCB itself,
  that the T10 tablet lists OTG support and runs Android 16);
  JCB Toughphone Pro-Smart Review, SlashGear
  (slashgear.com/jcb-toughphone-pro-smart-review-21229122/ —
  the 2012 Toughphone Pro-Smart TP909 runs Android 2.3.5
  Gingerbread on an 800 MHz single-core processor with 256 MB
  of ROM. A review, not a guide — and the confirmation that
  the old Pro-Smart runs Android 2.3.5, far below SnapDisk's
  Android 7.0 minimum, so it cannot run SnapDisk at all);
  Photos to USB: OTG Backup (Play listing,
  com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the one-tap app alternative:
  copies the whole gallery to a pen drive in one tap, copies
  only new photos next time, warns about the FAT32 4 GB limit.
  Free with ads, one-time purchase to remove them. Generic
  Android, not a JCB guide, and it does not promise
  oldest-first order, detailed progress with Pause and Stop,
  or size verification of every file); PhotoSaver Phone-to-Drive
  (Play listing, com.clevx.photosaver — a second one-tap app
  alternative: save photos and videos to any USB-C drive with
  one click, no cloud, no upload. Generic Android, not a JCB
  guide — but it does work on the current JCB models, since
  all of them have USB-C); cloud routes — Google Photos syncs
  the gallery to Google's servers, Google Drive backs data up
  to Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and Google backup restores
  data to a new phone from Google's cloud, not to a drive you
  hold; Google Photos' "Storage saver" setting compresses
  photos, and a cloud backup stops working the day you stop
  paying for storage. Neither route gives a folder of plain
  photo files on a USB drive.
  JCB specifics: OTG is confirmed in the sources found on the
  Toughphone, the Toughphone Max, the E10, and the T10 —
  cashify lists "OTG Support, Yes" for the Toughphone and the
  Toughphone Max, the E10 review lists "USB-C 2.0 (OTG)", and
  JCB's own T10 page lists "USB-C with OTG support" — and
  every current model can run SnapDisk: the Toughphone runs
  Android 13, the Toughphone Max runs Android 12, the E10
  runs Android 15, the E50 runs Android 16, and the T10 tablet
  runs Android 16; on the E50 no source found mentions OTG at
  all, so the article tells owners of that model to plug a
  drive in and check Settings > Storage before relying on it
  instead of claiming OTG across the range. Two old JCB phones
  cannot run SnapDisk at all: the TP802 is a feature phone,
  not an Android phone, and the Toughphone Pro-Smart TP909
  from 2012 runs Android 2.3.5, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0
  minimum. The E10's specs are disputed between sources: one
  review's own spec table says Android 16 while its text and
  JCB's own site say Android 15, its chipset is a Helio G36
  per that review against an MT6765X per Clove and
  rugged.co.uk, and its RAM is 4 GB per that review against
  8 GB per rugged.co.uk — so the chipset and RAM are not leaned
  on in the article, and the Android version is taken as 15
  from JCB's own site. All current models have USB-C. All
  current models take a microSD card — up to 1 TB on the
  Toughphone, the Toughphone Max, and the E50, up to 512 GB on
  the E10, and up to 2 TB on the T10 tablet.
  JCB's own tools: the only JCB file advice found is the old
  TP802 manual's PC copy with a USB cable (Mass Storage); the
  stock photo backup is Google Photos, and the stock file
  manager is Files by Google; a microSD card is the local
  option on every current model; no JCB backup page points at
  a USB drive at all — none of them writes plain photo files
  to a USB drive automatically. Out of scope, named in the
  article: OTG support is unconfirmed on the E50, so owners of
  that model should check Settings > Storage before relying
  on it; the E10's specs are disputed between sources
  (Android 15/16, Helio G36 vs MT6765X, 4 GB vs 8 GB of RAM);
  the TP802 and the Toughphone Pro-Smart TP909 cannot run
  SnapDisk (feature phone / Android 2.3.5) — SnapDisk needs
  Android 7.0 or later.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-90, 59th how-to):
  docs/jcb-photos-to-usb.html. Query 58 scored in
  tool/growth/searches.md. Blog index (JSON-LD + post block),
  sitemap.xml, llms.txt (incl. the Blog post list), llms-full.txt,
  feed.xml (59 items, lastBuildDate Nov 18) updated. Article PR
  #148, commit 896165f, CI run 44382520014 (build, success).
  Merge SHA 9a33b52887fb665e5f3df93f7f1db67a36be4228.
  Live verification (article 200 with correct title, feed 59
  items incl. JCB, sitemap 200 incl. the new URL, blog home
  200) re-checked after Pages deploy.
  This row is on a separate branch (wl-90-jcb-worklog) so
  the article PR stays clean; worklog PR to follow.
  Fallback for WL-91: Evolveo — no Evolveo article exists in
  docs/ yet.

  (coolmuster.com/android/transfer-photos-from-android-to-usb-flash-drive.html
  — drag and drop through a computer, or an OTG adapter with the
  file manager. Generic Android, and a manual guide — the first
  route needs a PC in between, and it notes that not all Android
  devices support OTG); Land Rover Explore user manual (English
  — 762 pages), manuals.co.uk
  (manuals.co.uk/land-rover/explore/manual?p=602 — the official
  manual: personal settings back up to Google's servers with a
  Google account, photos are viewed in the Google Photos app, and
  music files move from a PC onto the phone with the USB power
  cable. A manual, not a backup guide — and the confirmation
  that Land Rover's own backup advice points at Google's servers
  or a computer, never at a USB drive); Land Rover Explore user
  manual, gebruikershandleiding.com
  (gebruikershandleiding.com/LandRover-Explore/preview-handleiding-841917.html?page=0174
  — the same 762-page manual: synchronising information is
  through Google accounts, and files move between the phone and
  a PC with the USB cable. A manual, not a backup guide — and the
  confirmation that the same computer-or-cloud advice is the only
  backup advice Land Rover's pages give for the Explore);
  Land Rover Explore Smartphone Review, Notebookcheck
  (notebookcheck.net/Land-Rover-Explore-Smartphone-Review.320806.0.html
  — pure Android 7.1.1, with an update to Android 8 to follow;
  the USB Type-C connection transfers data at USB 2.0 speeds and
  "supports USB OTG". A review, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the Explore lists OTG support and runs
  Android 7.1); Land Rover Explore smartphone, FindPare
  (findpare.com/smartphone/land-rover-explore — "USB OTG: Yes",
  "USB Type C: Yes", Android 7, MediaTek Helio X27 (MT6797X),
  4 GB of RAM, 64 GB of storage. A spec table, not a guide —
  and a second source for the Explore's OTG support. FindPare
  itself says its information is not ascertained); Land Rover
  Explore R, mobiles4sale
  (mobiles4sale.com/models/land-rover-explore-r — Android 9 with
  upgrade to 10, MediaTek Helio P35 MT6765, 4 GB of RAM, 64 GB of
  storage, USB-C port, 3,100 mAh battery. A spec page, not a
  guide — and the confirmation that the Explore R runs Android 9,
  with no OTG mention); Land Rover Explore R Review, UL
  benchmarks
  (benchmarks.ul.com/zh-hans-jp/hardware/phone/Land+Rover+Explore+R+review
  — Android 9.0, Helio P35, 4 GB of RAM, 64 GB of storage, 12 MP
  camera, USB Type-C, microSD / microSDHC / microSDXC. A
  benchmark page, not a guide — and a second source for the
  Explore R's Android 9.0, with no OTG mention); Land Rover's
  New Explore R Smartphone, outdoorsmagic
  (outdoorsmagic.com/article/land-rover-explore-r-phone-first-look/
  — Android 9 (with upgrade to 10), 4 GB RAM, 64 GB ROM,
  expandable storage (microSD), dual SIM with nano SIM and
  microSD. A first-look article, not a guide — and a third source
  for the Explore R's Android 9, with no OTG mention); Land Rover
  launches Explore R ruggedized smartphone, Notebookcheck
  (notebookcheck.net/Land-Rover-launches-Explore-R-ruggedized-smartphone.490991.0.html
  — IP68, MIL-STD 810H, 5.7-inch HD+ display with Gorilla Glass
  6, 4 GB of RAM, 64 GB of storage expandable with a microSD card,
  3,100 mAh battery, shipping at £399. A launch story, not a
  guide — and the confirmation that the Explore R is the 2020
  successor with a bigger screen and a smaller battery than the
  original); Land Rover Explore Android Smartphone With Battery,
  Adventure Packs Launched, Gadgets360
  (gadgets360.com/mobiles/news/land-rover-explore-smartphone-battery-adventure-packs-price-specifications-1816305
  — 5-inch full-HD display with Gorilla Glass 5, Android Nougat
  with an upgrade to Android Oreo expected, 2.6 GHz deca-core
  MediaTek X27, 4 GB of RAM, 64 GB of storage expandable via
  microSD, 4000 mAh battery, IP68. A launch story, not a guide —
  and the confirmation that the original Explore runs Android 7
  with 64 GB of storage and a 4000 mAh battery); Photos to USB:
  OTG Backup (Play listing, com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the
  one-tap app alternative: copies the whole gallery to a pen drive
  in one tap, copies only new photos next time, warns about the
  FAT32 4 GB limit. Free with ads, one-time purchase to remove
  them. Generic Android, not a Land Rover Explore guide, and it
  does not promise oldest-first order, detailed progress with
  Pause and Stop, or size verification of every file);
  PhotoSaver Phone-to-Drive (Play listing, com.clevx.photosaver
  — a second one-tap app alternative: save photos and videos to
  any USB-C drive with one click, no cloud, no upload. Generic
  Android, not a Land Rover Explore guide — but it does work on
  both Explore models, since both have USB-C); cloud routes —
  Google Photos syncs the gallery to Google's servers, Google
  Drive backs data up to Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and
  Google backup restores data to a new phone from Google's cloud,
  not to a drive you hold; Google Photos' "Storage saver" setting
  compresses photos, and a cloud backup stops working the day you
  stop paying for storage. Neither route gives a folder of plain
  photo files on a USB drive.
  Land Rover specifics: OTG is confirmed in the sources found
  only on the original Explore — FindPare lists "USB OTG: Yes"
  and Notebookcheck's review says the USB-C connection "supports
  USB OTG" — and the Explore runs Android 7.1, so it can run
  SnapDisk; on the Explore R no source found mentions OTG at all,
  so the article tells owners of that model to plug a drive in and
  check Settings > Storage before relying on it instead of
  claiming OTG across the range. Both models can run SnapDisk:
  the Explore runs Android 7.1 and the Explore R runs Android 9
  with an upgrade to 10. One spec page disagrees about the
  Explore R: mobilespecs.net lists it as Android 7.0 with a
  Helio X27 — the original Explore's spec — against Android 9
  with a Helio P35 per mobiles4sale, UL benchmarks, and
  outdoorsmagic, so that page is not used as a source for the
  Explore R. Both models have USB-C. Both models take a microSD
  card, but the capacity is unlisted in the sources found.
  Land Rover's own tools: the Explore manual's backup is Google
  backup to Google's servers, its photo backup is Google Photos,
  and its file advice is a computer copy with a USB cable; a
  microSD card is the local option on both models; no Land Rover
  backup page points at a USB drive at all — none of them writes
  plain photo files to a USB drive automatically. Out of scope,
  named in the article: OTG support is unconfirmed on the
  Explore R, so owners of that model should check Settings >
  Storage before relying on it; one spec page (mobilespecs.net)
  disagrees about the Explore R (Android 7.0 / Helio X27 against
  Android 9 / Helio P35 per three other sources); the microSD
  capacity is unlisted in the sources found — SnapDisk needs
  Android 7.0 or later.

  (sunsky-online.com/p/WMC0843B/CONQUEST-S22-5G-Walkie-Talkie-Rugged-Phone-Tablet-Thermal-Imaging-6GB-128GB-Dual-Back-Cameras-Night-Vision-Camera-IP68-IP69K-Waterproof-Dustproof-Shockproof-Face-ID-&-Fingerprint-Identification-8.0-inch-Android-11-MTK6873-Dimensity-800-Octa-Core-up-to-2.0GHz-Network-5G-NFC-PoC-Black-.htm
  — Android 11, MTK6873 Dimensity 800, 6 GB of RAM with 128 GB
  of storage, 15200 mAh. A store listing, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the S22 5G runs Android 11, with no USB port

  listing, com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the one-tap app
  alternative: copies the whole gallery to a pen drive in one
  tap, copies only new photos next time, warns about the FAT32
  4 GB limit. Free with ads, one-time purchase to remove them.
  Generic Android, not a Conquest guide, and it does not promise
  oldest-first order, detailed progress with Pause and Stop,
  or size verification of every file); PhotoSaver
  Phone-to-Drive (Play listing, com.clevx.photosaver — a second
  one-tap app alternative: save photos and videos to any USB-C
  drive with one click, no cloud, no upload. Generic Android,
  not a Conquest guide, and it needs a USB-C connection, so it
  is no use on the micro-USB Conquest models, only on the S12
  and the S16, which are the Conquest models found with USB-C);
  cloud routes — Google Photos syncs the gallery to Google's
  servers, Google Drive backs data up to Google's cloud with
  15 GB free, and Google backup restores data to a new phone
  from Google's cloud, not to a drive you hold; Google Photos'
  "Storage saver" setting compresses photos, and a cloud backup
  stops working the day you stop paying for storage. Neither
  route gives a folder of plain photo files on a USB drive.
  Conquest specifics: OTG is confirmed in the sources found only
  on the S8 — FindPare lists "USB OTG: Yes" with micro USB 2.0
  — and the S8 runs Android 6.0, so it cannot run SnapDisk; on
  the S10, the S12, the S12 Pro, the S16, the S22 5G, and the
  EX201, no source found mentions OTG at all, so the article
  tells owners of those models to plug a drive in and check
  Settings > Storage before relying on it instead of claiming
  OTG across the range. Four models cannot run SnapDisk at all:
  the S6 runs Android 5.1, and the S8, the S8+, and the S8 Pro
  run Android 6.0, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, even
  though the S8 lists OTG support. The six models that can run
  SnapDisk run Android 7.1 (S10), 8.1 (S12, S12 Pro), 9.0 (S16),
  11 (S22 5G), and 12 (EX201). The S9's Android version is
  disputed (5.1.1 per devicesfaq, 7.1.1 per trak.in), and its
  chipset is disputed too (Snapdragon 615 per devicesfaq, Helio
  P25 MT6757CD per trak.in). The S6 comes in two variants
  (MT6732, 1 GB RAM, 8 GB storage, 4000 mAh per Ubergizmo
  against MT6752, 3 GB RAM, 32 GB storage, 6000 mAh for the S6
  OCTA per mobilexfiles), though both run Android 5.1. The USB
  port type is unconfirmed on the S22 5G and the EX201 — the S10
  has micro USB, the S12 and the S16 have USB-C, and the S8 has
  micro USB 2.0. The S8+ takes a microSD card up to 128 GB, the
  S10 takes one up to 128 GB, and the S16 takes one up to
  128 GB (ATEX variant up to 2 TB) — but a card is still not a
  USB drive: it stays in the phone and opening it anywhere else
  needs a card reader.
  Conquest's own tools: devicesfaq's backup advice for the S8 (a
  computer copy with a USB cable and a file browser, or Google
  Drive with 15 GB free and Google Photos in the cloud) points
  at a computer or the cloud, never at a USB drive, and the same
  template appears on the S8 KT35A-S8 page; Google Photos (the
  stock photo backup on Conquest Android phones) backs photos up
  to Google's cloud; Google Drive (Android's default built-in
  service, 15 GB free) backs data up to Google's cloud; a
  microSD card (up to 128 GB on the S8+, up to 128 GB on the
  S10, up to 128 GB on the S16) is a real local copy, but the
  card stays in the phone and needs a reader elsewhere; a
  computer copy (connect the phone to a computer using the USB
  cable and make a copy of the files using the file browser)
  lands on the PC, not on a USB drive; no Conquest backup page
  points at a USB drive at all — none of them writes plain photo
  files to a USB drive automatically. Out of scope, named in the
  article: OTG support is unconfirmed on the S10, the S12, the
  S12 Pro, the S16, the S22 5G, and the EX201, so owners of
  those models should check Settings > Storage before relying
  on it; the S6, the S8, the S8+, and the S8 Pro run Android 5.1
  or 6.0, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, so SnapDisk
  cannot run on them; the S9 Android version is disputed (5.1.1
  per devicesfaq, 7.1.1 per trak.in) and its chipset is disputed
  (Snapdragon 615 per devicesfaq, Helio P25 MT6757CD per
  trak.in); the S6 comes in two variants (MT6732 per Ubergizmo,
  MT6752 for the S6 OCTA per mobilexfiles); the USB port type is
  unconfirmed on the S22 5G and the EX201 — SnapDisk needs
  Android 7.0 or later.

  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/nomu-s30-mini

  (phonemore.com/compare/phones/nomu-s10-pro-vs-samsung-galaxy-s20-fe-5g-sm-g781bds-128gb8gb/24882853

  (phonemore.com/compare/phones/nomu-s10-vs-quantum-sky/6590257

  — Android 6.0 Marshmallow, 64 GB of storage, microSD up to

  storage, 3000 mAh. A spec table, not a guide — and a second

  (phonesdata.com/en/smartphones/nomu/nomu-m6-5460419/ —
  Android 7.0 Nougat, MediaTek MT6737T, 2 GB of RAM, 16 GB of
  storage, "USB: Yes, USB On-The-Go (OTG)", 3000 mAh, launched

  confirmation that the M6 lists OTG and runs Android 7.0);
  Nomu M6 Smartphone Review, Notebookcheck
  (notebookcheck.net/Nomu-M6-Smartphone-Review.299774.0.html —
  "The Nomu M6 is equipped with a Micro-USB port supporting
  the transfer standard 2.0. The port is unable to output

  the M6's Android 7.0 and OTG, against unite4buy's microSD

  (phonesdata.com/en/smartphones/nomu/nomu-m8-5460417/ —
  Android 7.0 Nougat, MediaTek MT6750T, 4 GB of RAM, 64 GB of

  confirmation that the M8 is the only Nomu model I found with
  USB-C and OTG); Nomu M8, devicesfaq
  (devicesfaq.com/en/characteristics/nomu-m8 — Android 7.0
  Nougat, MediaTek MT6750T, 4 GB of RAM, 64 GB of storage,
  "USB: USB Type-C", 2950 mAh. A spec table, not a guide — and
  a second source for the M8's Android 7.0 and USB-C, with no
  OTG mention on this page and a 2950 mAh battery against
  unite4buy's 3000 mAh, so the M8 battery is disputed); Nomu
  T18, gizmochina
  (gizmochina.com/product/nomu-t-18-smartphone-full-specification-and-features/
  — Android 7.0 Nougat, MediaTek MT6737, 3 GB of RAM, 32 GB of
  storage, microSD up to 32 GB, "Micro USB 2.0 With OTG,
  Charging and Mass Storage Function", 5200 mAh, released
  November 18, 2017. A spec table, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the T18 lists OTG and runs Android 7.0);
  Photos to USB: OTG Backup (Play listing,
  com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the one-tap app alternative:
  copies the whole gallery to a pen drive in one tap, copies
  only new photos next time, warns about the FAT32 4 GB limit.
  Free with ads, one-time purchase to remove them. Generic
  Android, not a Nomu guide, and it does not promise
  oldest-first order, detailed progress with Pause and Stop,
  or size verification of every file); PhotoSaver
  Phone-to-Drive (Play listing, com.clevx.photosaver — a second
  one-tap app alternative: save photos and videos to any USB-C
  drive with one click, no cloud, no upload. Generic Android,
  not a Nomu guide, and it needs a USB-C connection, so it is
  no use on the microUSB Nomu models, only on the M8, which is
  the one Nomu model with USB-C); cloud routes — Google Photos
  syncs the gallery to Google's servers, Google Drive backs
  data up to Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and Google backup
  restores data to a new phone from Google's cloud, not to a
  drive you hold; Google Photos' "Storage saver" setting
  compresses photos, and a cloud backup stops working the day
  you stop paying for storage. Neither route gives a folder of
  plain photo files on a USB drive.
  Nomu specifics: OTG is listed for seven of the eight models
  I found — the S10 Pro, the S20, the S30, the S30 Mini, the
  M6, the M8, and the T18 — and unconfirmed only on the S10,
  where PhoneMore lists microUSB 2.0 with no OTG mention, so
  the article tells owners of that model to check Settings >
  Storage before relying on it instead of claiming OTG across
  the range. Three models cannot run SnapDisk at all: the S10,
  the S20, and the S30 run Android 6.0, below SnapDisk's
  Android 7.0 minimum, even though the S20 and the S30 list
  OTG support. The five models that can run SnapDisk all run
  Android 7.0: the S10 Pro, the S30 Mini, the M6, the M8, and
  the T18. The M8 is the only Nomu model I found with a USB-C
  port — every other model has microUSB 2.0. The specs are
  disputed between sources on several models: the S20 chipset
  (MediaTek MT6735 per Review Hub, MT6737T per UL benchmarks);
  the T18 chipset (MT6737 per gizmochina, MT6737T per
  unite4buy); the M8 Android version (7.0 per phonesdata and
  devicesfaq, 7.1 per unite4buy) and battery (2950 mAh per
  devicesfaq, 3000 mAh per unite4buy); the microSD size on the
  S10 Pro (up to 64 GB per PhoneMore, up to 32 GB per
  Smartprix), the S30 (up to 64 GB per PhoneMore, up to 32 GB
  per Notebookcheck), and the M6 (up to 32 GB per Notebookcheck,
  up to 64 GB per unite4buy). The S10 takes a microSD card up
  to 64 GB, the S30 Mini takes one up to 64 GB, and the T18
  takes one up to 32 GB — but a card is still not a USB drive:
  it stays in the phone and opening it anywhere else needs a
  card reader.
  Nomu's own tools: devicesfaq's backup advice for the S10 (a
  computer copy with a USB cable and a file browser, or Google
  Drive with 15 GB free and Google Photos in the cloud) points
  at a computer or the cloud, never at a USB drive, and the same
  template appears on the S30 Mini page; Google Photos (the
  stock photo backup on Nomu Android phones) backs photos up to
  Google's cloud; Google Drive (Android's default built-in
  service, 15 GB free) backs data up to Google's cloud; a
  microSD card (up to 64 GB on the S10, up to 64 GB on the S30
  Mini, up to 32 GB on the T18) is a real local copy, but the
  card stays in the phone and needs a reader elsewhere; a
  computer copy (connect the phone to a computer using the USB
  cable and make a copy of the files using the file browser)
  lands on the PC, not on a USB drive; no Nomu backup page
  points at a USB drive at all — none of them writes plain photo
  files to a USB drive automatically. Out of scope, named in the
  article: OTG support is unconfirmed on the S10, so owners of
  that model should check Settings > Storage before relying on
  it; the S10, the S20, and the S30 run Android 6.0, below
  SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum, so SnapDisk cannot run on
  them; the S20 chipset is disputed (MT6735 per Review Hub,
  MT6737T per UL benchmarks); the T18 chipset is disputed
  (MT6737 per gizmochina, MT6737T per unite4buy); the M8
  Android version is disputed (7.0 per phonesdata and
  devicesfaq, 7.1 per unite4buy) and its battery is disputed
  (2950 mAh per devicesfaq, 3000 mAh per unite4buy); the
  microSD size is disputed on the S10 Pro (64 GB per PhoneMore,
  32 GB per Smartprix), the S30 (64 GB per PhoneMore, 32 GB per
  Notebookcheck), and the M6 (32 GB per Notebookcheck, 64 GB
  per unite4buy); the M8 is the only Nomu model with USB-C, so
  owners of the microUSB models should buy a micro-USB OTG
  adapter, not a USB-C one — SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or
  later.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
