  (reviewcentralme.com/2020/03/04/energizer-launches-new-rugged-phone-hard-case-h280s/
  — "Avenir Telecom, under the exclusive Energizer brand
  license, announces the release of its new rugged phone, the
  Hard Case H280S. … Running KaiOS, it provides access to
  hundreds of applications and has 4G LTE connectivity." A launch
  report, not a guide — and the confirmation that Avenir Telecom
  makes the Hard Case phones under license and that the H280S
  runs KaiOS); Energizer Hardcase H242S, phonesdata
  (phonesdata.com/en/smartphones/energizer/hardcase-h242s-5641/
  — KaiOS, Spreadtrum SC7731E, 512 MB of storage, 256 MB of RAM,
  2.4-inch display, 0.3 MP camera, announced February 2019. A
  spec table, not a guide — and the confirmation that the H242S
  runs KaiOS, not Android); Energizer Hardcase H242S, zakphone
  (zakphone.com/phone/energizer-hardcase-h242s/ — KaiOS 2.5.1,
  Spreadtrum SC7731E, 512 MB of storage, 256 MB of RAM,
  "Announced Date 2019, February", "Status Information
  Cancelled". A spec table, not a guide — and the confirmation
  that the H242S runs KaiOS 2.5.1 and was cancelled); Energizer
  Hardcase H240S, Gadgets360
  (gadgets360.com/energizer-hardcase-h240s-4590/amp — Android
  6.0, MediaTek MT6737M, 1 GB of RAM, 8 GB of storage, microSD
  up to 32 GB, 2000 mAh. A spec table, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the H240S runs Android 6.0, below SnapDisk's
  minimum); Energizer Hardcase H240S Feature Phone With 4G VoLTE
  Support Launched, Gadgets360
  (gadgets360.com/mobiles/news/energizer-hardcase-h240s-4g-feature-phone-1803739
  — "Avenir Telecom, a licensee of the Energizer brand, launched
  a new feature phone on Tuesday. The Energizer Hardcase H240S is
  a 4G feature phone. Notably, the phone runs Android, but
  without access to Google Play - users will have to settle for
  the pre-installed apps on board." A launch report, not a guide
  — and the confirmation that the H240S runs Android 6.0 with no
  Google Play access); Energizer Ultimate U620S Pop, GSMArena
  (m.gsmarena.com/energizer_ultimate_u620s_pop-9548.php —
  "Cancelled … Android 9.0 … 128GB storage, microSDXC", "USB:
  USB Type-C 2.0, OTG". A spec table, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the only Energizer phone I found with an OTG
  listing is a cancelled phone from the Ultimate line, not a Hard
  Case phone); Photos to USB: OTG Backup (Play listing,
  com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the one-tap app alternative: copies
  the whole gallery to a pen drive in one tap, copies only new
  photos next time, warns about the FAT32 4 GB limit. Free with
  ads, one-time purchase to remove them. Generic Android, not an
  Energizer guide, and it does not promise oldest-first order,
  detailed progress with Pause and Stop, or size verification of
  every file); PhotoSaver Phone-to-Drive (Play listing,
  com.clevx.photosaver — a second one-tap app alternative: save
  photos and videos to any USB-C drive with one click, no cloud,
  no upload. Generic Android, not an Energizer guide, and it
  needs a USB-C connection, so it is no use on the microUSB Hard
  Case models); cloud routes — Google Photos syncs the gallery
  to Google's servers, Google Drive backs data up to Google's
  cloud with 15 GB free, and Google backup restores data to a new
  phone from Google's cloud, not to a drive you hold; Google
  Photos' "Storage saver" setting compresses photos, and a cloud
  backup stops working the day you stop paying for storage.
  Neither route gives a folder of plain photo files on a USB
  drive.
  Energizer specifics: OTG is unconfirmed across the whole Hard
  Case line — no Hard Case model lists USB OTG support in the
  sources I found; the spec tables list USB Type-C for the H550S,
  the H570S, the H590S, and the H620S and microUSB 2.0 for the
  H500S, with no OTG mention on any of them — so the article
  tells owners of every model to check Settings > Storage before
  relying on it instead of claiming OTG across the range; the
  only Energizer OTG listing found is the cancelled Ultimate
  U620S Pop, a different line that was never sold. Two models
  cannot run SnapDisk at all: the H280S and the H242S run KaiOS,
  not Android, and the H240S runs Android 6.0 with no Google Play
  access, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum. The H550S runs
  Android 7.0, exactly the minimum; the H590S runs Android 8.0,
  the H620S runs Android 10, and the H621S runs Android 11; the
  H621S connector is unverified in the sources I found. The
  H570S specs are disputed between sources (Android 8.0, Helio
  P18, 3 GB RAM, 32 GB storage, microSD up to 128 GB, 4800 mAh
  per Gadgets360's 2018 launch story, against Android 9.0,
  MediaTek MT6739, 2 GB RAM, 16 GB storage, microSD up to 32 GB,
  6500 mAh per phonesdata, specspro, and others); the H500S
  Android version is disputed (Android 8.0 per pkgsm, Android
  7.0 per mobilespecs and spec411); the H550S takes a microSD
  card up to 32 GB, the H500S takes one up to 64 GB, and the
  H240S takes one up to 32 GB — but a card is still not a USB
  drive: it stays in the phone and opening it anywhere else needs
  a card reader.
  Energizer's own tools: devicesfaq's backup advice for the
  H550S (a computer copy with a USB cable and a file browser, or
  Google Drive with 15 GB free and Google Photos in the cloud)
  points at a computer or the cloud, never at a USB drive, and
  the same template appears on the H590S, H620S, H501S, H240S,
  H280S, and H242 pages; Google Photos (the stock photo backup on
  Energizer Hard Case Android phones) backs photos up to Google's
  cloud; Google Drive (Android's default built-in service, 15 GB
  free) backs data up to Google's cloud; a microSD card (up to
  32 GB on the H550S, up to 64 GB on the H500S, up to 32 GB on
  the H240S) is a real local copy, but the card stays in the
  phone and needs a reader elsewhere; a computer copy (connect
  the phone to a computer using the USB cable and make a copy of
  the files using the file browser) lands on the PC, not on a USB
  drive; no Energizer backup page points at a USB drive at all —
  none of them writes plain photo files to a USB drive
  automatically. Out of scope, named in the article: OTG host
  support is unconfirmed on every Hard Case model, so owners of
  every model should check Settings > Storage before relying on
  it; the H280S and the H242S run KaiOS, not Android, so SnapDisk
  cannot run on them; the H240S runs Android 6.0 with no Google
  Play access, below SnapDisk's Android 7.0 minimum; the H570S
  specs are disputed between sources (Android 8.0 / Helio P18 /
  3 GB / 32 GB / 4800 mAh per Gadgets360, against Android 9.0 /
  MT6739 / 2 GB / 16 GB / 6500 mAh per phonesdata and specspro);
  the H500S Android version is disputed (8.0 per pkgsm, 7.0 per
  mobilespecs and spec411); the H621S connector is unverified in
  the sources above, so owners of that model should check their
  own port before buying an adapter — SnapDisk needs Android 7.0
  or later.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.

  com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the one-tap app alternative: copies

  the gallery to Google's servers, Google Drive backs data up to
  Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and Google backup restores data
  to a new phone from Google's cloud, not to a drive you hold;
  Google Photos' "Storage saver" setting compresses photos, and
  a cloud backup stops working the day you stop paying for
  storage. Neither route gives a folder of plain photo files on a
  USB drive.
  UMIDIGI specifics: OTG is confirmed on most of the line — the
  spec tables list USB OTG as Yes for the Bison, the Bison Pro,
  the Bison Plus, the Bison 2 Pro, the Bison X10, the Bison X10G,
  and the Bison X10 Pro — but the Bison X20's OTG host support is
  unverified (one source lists OTG Support as No, the others list
  USB Type-C only, and the reviews mention OTG reverse charging
  only) and the Bison GT, the Bison GT2 Pro, the Bison GT2 Pro
  5G, the Bison X10S, and the Bison X10S NFC have no OTG listing
  in the sources I found, so the article tells owners of those
  models to check Settings > Storage before relying on it instead
  of claiming OTG across the range; the original Bison's
  connector is disputed between sources (USB-C per GSMArena and
  MySmartPrice, microUSB per mobility.com.ng), so the article
  tells owners to check their own port before buying an adapter;
  all seven named models have USB-C per the spec tables, so a
  USB-C flash drive plugs straight in and a regular USB-A drive
  needs a USB-C OTG adapter; the Bison Pro takes a microSD card
  up to 256 GB, the Bison 2 Pro takes one up to 512 GB, and the
  Bison X20 takes one up to 1 TB — the original Bison's card size
  is the one disagreement in the sources, 256 GB per MySmartPrice
  and 128 GB per mobility.com.ng — but a card is still not a USB
  drive: it stays in the phone and opening it anywhere else needs
  a card reader.
  UMIDIGI's own tools: devicesfaq's backup advice for the Bison
  (a computer copy with a USB cable and a file browser, or Google
  Drive with 15 GB free and Google Photos in the cloud) points at
  a computer or the cloud, never at a USB drive, and the same
  template appears on the Bison GT, Bison Pro, Bison X10, Bison

  the Bison X20 reviews' OTG mention is reverse charging only;
  Google Photos (the stock photo backup on UMIDIGI Bison Android
  phones) backs photos up to Google's cloud; Google Drive
  (Android's default built-in service, 15 GB free) backs data up
  to Google's cloud; a microSD card (up to 256 GB on the Bison
  Pro, up to 512 GB on the Bison 2 Pro, up to 1 TB on the Bison
  X20) is a real local copy, but the card stays in the phone and
  needs a reader elsewhere; a computer copy (connect the phone to
  a computer using the USB cable and make a copy of the files
  using the file browser) lands on the PC, not on a USB drive; no
  UMIDIGI backup page points at a USB drive at all — none of them
  writes plain photo files to a USB drive automatically. Out of
  scope, named in the article: the Bison X20's OTG host support
  is unverified (one source lists OTG Support as No, the others
  list USB Type-C only, and the reviews mention reverse charging
  only); the Bison GT, the Bison GT2 Pro, the Bison GT2 Pro 5G,
  the Bison X10S, and the Bison X10S NFC have no OTG listing in
  the sources I found, so their OTG support is unverified; the
  Bison GT2 Pro 5G's Android version is disputed between sources
  (Android 11 per devicesfaq, Android 12 per gsmfind); the
  original Bison's connector is disputed (USB-C per GSMArena and
  MySmartPrice, microUSB per mobility.com.ng); the Bison X10,
  X10G, and X10 Pro have unverified Android versions in the
  sources above, so owners of those models should check Settings
  > About phone — SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later.

  — "Copy local originals to compatible USB storage, open the

  (softhandtech.com/can-you-transfer-photos-from-phone-to-memory-stick/

  phone's battery and is not the same as hosting a drive);
  FOSSIBOT F107 Pro Rugged Phone User Manual, manuals.plus
  (manuals.plus/asin/B0FGQB7T67.pdf — Android 15, 512 GB of
  storage expandable to 2 TB, a 28000 mAh battery, and "10W OTG

  own OTG mention is reverse charging only, while the
  official-site spec is the one that names flash drives over an

  (geekbuying.com/item/FOSSiBOT-F101-PRO-Rugged-Smartphone-Orange-522632.html
  — Android 13.0, "OTG: Support", a TF card up to 1 TB, and a

  (rottenwifi.com/fossibot-f102-full-specifications-price-and-features/
  — Android 13, 256 GB of storage with "microSD/TF expansion up
  to 1TB", and "OTG Reverse Charge" on the 16500 mAh battery. A
  review, not a guide — and the confirmation that the F102 runs
  Android 13, with the card size disagreeing with Cashify's
  2 TB); F115 Ultra Review, SpecsBattle
  (specsbattle.com/phones/fossibot-f115-ultra — "Connectivity —
  USB: USB charging and USB host supported; USB-C listed as
  false; USB OTG listed as false", and "That combination is
  unusual. Buyers should confirm the port type and accessory
  support before purchasing." A spec-comparison page, not a guide
  — and the confirmation that the F115 Ultra's spec data
  conflicts, so its OTG support is unverified); Frequently asked
  questions FOSSiBOT F102, devicesfaq
  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/fossibot-f102 —
  "How to backup my FOSSiBOT F102? … The most economical method
  to make a backup is to connect the FOSSiBOT F102 to a computer
  using the USB cable and make a copy of the files using the file
  browser." — and photos via Google Photos, with downloads in
  "Files", previously known as "Files by Google". A FAQ page, not
  a backup guide — and the confirmation that FOSSiBOT's own
  backup advice points at a computer or the cloud, never at a USB
  drive); How to update the software for FOSSiBOT F101,
  devicesfaq (devicesfaq.com/en/update/fossibot-f101 — "Your
  FOSSiBOT F101 went on sale in October 2020, 6 years ago." An
  update guide, not a backup guide — and the confirmation that
  the original F101 is the oldest F-series model I found); Photos
  to USB: OTG Backup (Play listing, com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb —
  the one-tap app alternative: copies the whole gallery to a pen
  drive in one tap, copies only new photos next time, warns about
  the FAT32 4 GB limit. Free with ads, one-time purchase to
  remove them. Generic Android, not a FOSSiBOT guide, and it does
  not promise oldest-first order, detailed progress with Pause
  and Stop, or size verification of every file); cloud routes —
  Google Photos syncs the gallery to Google's servers, Google
  Drive backs data up to Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and
  Google backup restores data to a new phone from Google's cloud,
  not to a drive you hold; Google Photos' "Storage saver" setting
  compresses photos, and a cloud backup stops working the day
  you stop paying for storage. Neither route gives a folder of
  plain photo files on a USB drive.
  FOSSiBOT specifics: OTG is confirmed on most of the line — the
  spec tables list USB OTG as Yes for the F101, the F101 PRO,
  the F102, the F110 Pro 5G, and the F113, the F112 Pro's spec
  page lists USB OTG Type-C, and the F107 Pro's official-site
  spec says you can connect USB flash drives via an OTG cable —
  but the F101P's official page mentions OTG only as reverse
  charging and the F115 Ultra's spec data conflicts, so the
  article tells owners of those two models to check Settings >
  Storage before relying on it instead of claiming OTG across
  the range; all seven named models have USB-C, so a USB-C flash
  drive plugs straight in and a regular USB-A drive needs a
  USB-C OTG adapter; the F101 Pro takes a microSD card up to
  1 TB, and the F107 Pro and the F113 take one up to 2 TB — the
  F102's card size is the one disagreement in the sources, 1 TB
  per RottenWifi and 2 TB per Cashify — but a card is still not a
  USB drive: it stays in the phone and opening it anywhere else
  needs a card reader.
  FOSSiBOT's own tools: devicesfaq's backup advice for the F102
  (a computer copy with a USB cable and a file browser, or Google
  Drive with 15 GB free and Google Photos in the cloud) points at
  a computer or the cloud, never at a USB drive; the F107 Pro
  manual's OTG mention is reverse charging only; Google Photos
  (the stock photo backup on FOSSiBOT Android phones) backs
  photos up to Google's cloud; Google Drive (Android's default
  built-in service, 15 GB free) backs data up to Google's cloud;
  a microSD card (up to 1 TB on the F101 Pro, up to 2 TB on the
  F107 Pro and the F113) is a real local copy, but the card stays
  in the phone and needs a reader elsewhere; a computer copy
  (connect the phone to a computer using the USB cable and make a
  copy of the files using the file browser) lands on the PC, not
  on a USB drive; no FOSSiBOT backup page points at a USB drive
  at all — none of them writes plain photo files to a USB drive
  automatically. Out of scope, named in the article: the F101P's
  OTG host support is unlisted (official page mentions OTG only
  as reverse charging); the F115 Ultra's spec data conflicts
  (USB OTG listed as false while USB-C is also listed as false),
  so its OTG support is unverified; the original F101 went on
  sale in October 2020 and its Android version is unverified in
  the sources above, so owners of that model should check
  Settings > About phone — SnapDisk needs Android 7.0 or later.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.

## WL-82 — Back up Crosscall photos to USB article, 51st how-to (DONE)

- Owner: SEO/GEO lane lead. State: DONE.
  New docs/crosscall-photos-to-usb.html ("Back up Crosscall photos to
  a USB drive (no computer)"), following the
  WL-54/WL-55/WL-56/WL-57/WL-58/WL-59/WL-60/WL-61/WL-62/WL-63/
  WL-64/WL-65/WL-66/WL-67/WL-68/WL-69/WL-70/WL-71/WL-72/WL-73/
  WL-74/WL-75/WL-76/WL-77/WL-78/WL-79/WL-80/WL-81 article template
  exactly (navy/cyan, TL;DR first, no JS, text-only, reuses
  docs/screenshots-01-settings-defaults.png as the lead shot —
  same reuse as the 8th through 50th articles). Facts verified
  from code on fresh main: SAF tree picker (MainActivity.kt
  OpenDocumentTree + takePersistableUriPermission); oldest-first
  via MediaStore DATE_TAKEN ascending (PhotoQuery.kt, DATE_TAKEN
  else DATE_MODIFIED fallback); free 5 GB per run
  (ProPrefs.FREE_BACKUP_BYTES_LIMIT); Pro $9.99 one-time =
  auto-backup on connect + unlimited size (strings.xml
  pro_price); no INTERNET permission in AndroidManifest.xml;
  FAT32 files >=4 GiB skipped with reason
  (FilesystemInfo.FAT32_MAX_FILE_BYTES); raw byte-stream copy
  (BackupEngine.copyWithResume — no decode/re-encode) with size
  verification after copy. Search evidence (Nov 18, 2026 live
  web search): there is no Crosscall-specific phone-to-USB guide
  in the top results at all — the Crosscall-specific results are
  spec tables, datasheets, user manuals, and FAQ pages that give
  no USB-drive advice at all, and the rest are generic manual
  guides. Android Central forum, "Moving photos to usb"
  (forums.androidcentral.com/threads/moving-photos-to-usb.973408/
  — "You can use the OTG Adapter to plug in any external device
  that's supported … and it should be visible as external storage
  in the File Manager … and you can move files to it from there."
  A forum thread, not a guide — and a manual file-manager copy);
  Pixoneye, "How to Transfer Photos from Your Phone to a USB
  Stick" (pixoneye.com/how-to-transfer-photos-from-phone-to-usb-stick/
  — "Copy local originals to compatible USB storage, open the
  files from the destination, then make an independent backup
  before deleting anything" — Android with a file manager, or a
  computer in between. Generic Android and iPhone, and a manual
  file-manager guide); askfilo, "how to transfer pictures from
  an android phone to a flashdrive"
  (askfilo.com/user-question-answers-smart-solutions/how-to-transfer-pictures-from-an-android-phone-to-a-3436303837303432
  — plug the flash drive into an OTG adapter, open the file
  manager, find the DCIM folder, and copy to the USB drive.
  Generic Android, and a manual file-manager guide); YouTube,
  "How to Connect USB Drive to Android Phone to Move Photos &
  Videos (Best Method)" (youtube.com/watch?v=PYcbV25CczI —
  OTG connection and the phone's built-in file manager, no
  computer or internet needed. Generic Android, and a manual
  file-manager guide); YouTube, "How To Move Photos From iPhone
  To USB Flash Drive (No Computer)"
  (youtube.com/watch?v=NJHeuEYJhto — move photos from an iPhone
  straight onto a USB flash drive, with no computer in between.
  An iPhone guide, not an Android one — and not a Crosscall
  guide); CellularNews, "How to Move Pictures to a Flash Drive
  From a Google Phone"
  (cellularnews.com/device-reviews-and-comparisons/smartphones/how-to-move-pictures-to-a-flash-drive-from-google-phone/
  — connect a USB storage device using an OTG adapter and then
  copy or move the photos using the Files app. A Google Pixel
  guide, not a Crosscall guide — and a manual file-manager
  copy); Dr.Fone, "4 Practical Ways to Transfer Photos from
  Android to USB Flash Drive"
  (drfone.wondershare.com/android-transfer/how-to-transfer-photos-from-android-to-usb.html
  — transfer photos with an OTG adapter, through Google Drive,
  by drag-and-drop through a PC, or wirelessly through AirDroid.
  Generic Android, and the direct route is a manual file-manager
  copy); Trustloud, "How to Back Up an Android Phone: 5 Free
  Ways (2026)" (trustloud.com/back-up-android-phone/ — "open
  Settings > Google > All services > Backup, turn on Backup by
  Google One" and turn on Backup in Google Photos; anyone can
  copy files to a Windows PC over USB for an offline copy. A
  generic Android backup guide, not a Crosscall guide — and the
  backup it describes is a cloud backup or a PC copy, never a
  phone-to-USB copy); Google Photos Help, "Back up photos &
  videos" (support.google.com/photos/answer/6193313 — "You can
  automatically save your photos and videos to your Google
  Account when you turn on backup." A Google help page, not a
  Crosscall guide — and a cloud backup, not a USB copy);
  Frequently asked questions Crosscall Action-X3, devicesfaq
  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/crosscall-crosscall-action-x3
  — "How to backup my CROSSCALL Crosscall Action-X3? … The most
  economical method to make a backup is to connect the CROSSCALL
  Crosscall Action-X3 to a computer using the USB cable and make
  a copy of the files using the file browser. To perform a cloud
  backup you need a cloud backup service, on Android devices the
  default built-in service is Google Drive. With Google Drive
  you have 15Gb free" — and photos via Google Photos. A FAQ
  page, not a backup guide — and the confirmation that
  Crosscall's own backup advice points at a computer or the
  cloud, never at a USB drive); Frequently asked questions
  Crosscall Trekker-M1, devicesfaq
  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/crosscall-crosscall-trekker-m1
  — the same backup advice again: a computer copy with a USB
  cable and a file browser, or a cloud backup through Google
  Drive (15 GB free), with photos via Google Photos. A FAQ
  page, not a guide); Crosscall Trekker-X4 manual, libble
  (libble.eu/crosscall-trekker-x4/online-manual-841915/?page=0058
  — "Using a USB cable, connect your device to a computer. Open
  the notification panel, then tap Device connected in charging
  mode > Transfer files. Press Transfer photos (PTP) if your
  computer does not support the Media Transfer Protocol (MTP)
  format." A user manual, not a backup guide — and the
  confirmation that the manual's own photo-transfer advice needs
  a computer); Crosscall CORE-X5 user manual, manuals.ca
  (manuals.ca/crosscall/core-x5/manual?p=56 — Android 11, 64 GB
  of storage, a microSD card up to 512 GB, "USB version 3.2 Gen
  1 (3.1 Gen 1)", and "USB On-The-Go support: Yes". A spec
  table, not a guide — and the confirmation that the Core-X5
  has OTG); Action-X5 product sheet, Crosscall
  (doc.crosscall.com/distributor-area/PRODUCT%20SHEETS/SMARTPHONES/ACTION-X5/EN/EN_ACTION-X5_FICHE_PRODUIT_V3.pdf
  — Android 11, "USB Type-C 2.0", and "OTG: Yes". Crosscall's
  own product sheet — and the confirmation that the Action-X5
  has OTG); Crosscall ACTION-X5, cartronics
  (cartronics.be/en/crosscall/22142-crosscall-action-x5-138-cm-545-dual-sim-android-11-4g-usb-type-c-4-gb-64-gb-3850-mah-black-3700764722220.html
  — Android 11, 64 GB of storage expandable to 512 GB, and "USB
  connector type: USB Type-C". A spec page, not a guide — and a
  second source for the Action-X5's Android 11 and 512 GB card);
  Crosscall CORE-Z5 product specification, AiO
  (aio.lv/en/product--crosscall-1001012001980--10956595/specification
  — "USB On-The-Go support: Yes", "USB version 3.2 Gen 1 (3.1
  Gen 1)", and "USB connector type: USB Type-C". A spec table,
  not a guide — and the confirmation that the Core-Z5 has OTG);
  Crosscall Core-Z5, benchmarks.ul.com
  (benchmarks.ul.com/de-jp/hardware/phone/Crosscall+Core-Z5+review
  — Android 12, 64 GB of storage, a microSD card, and a 48 MP
  camera. A spec page, not a guide — and the confirmation that
  the Core-Z5 runs Android 12); Crosscall CORE-Z5, quzo
  (quzo.co.uk/corez5-6gb-uk-1001013317561-pid382332.html — 64 GB
  of storage and a "Maximum memory card size 1 TB". A spec page,
  not a guide — and the confirmation that the Core-Z5 takes a
  microSD card up to 1 TB); Crosscall STELLAR-X5 user manual,
  manuals.ca (manuals.ca/crosscall/stellar-x5/manual?p=56 —
  Android 13, 128 GB of storage, and a "USB connector type: USB
  Type-C". A spec table, not a guide — and it does not list OTG
  either way); Crosscall Trekker M1 Core, benchmarks.ul.com
  (benchmarks.ul.com/de-pt/hardware/phone/Crosscall+Trekker+M1+Core+review
  — Android 5.1, a Snapdragon 210 processor, and a microUSB 2.0
  port. A spec page, not a guide — and the confirmation that
  the Trekker-M1 Core runs Android 5.1, below SnapDisk's Android
  7.0 minimum); Crosscall Trekker-M1 vs Punkt. MP02, versus.com
  (versus.com/en/crosscall-trekker-m1-vs-punkt-mp02 — the
  Trekker-M1 runs Android 5.1 Lollipop, released October 2016. A
  comparison page, not a guide — and a second source confirming
  the Trekker-M1 runs Android 5.1); Synchronize Crosscall
  Core-X4, PhoneCopy (phonecopy.com/en/phones/view/crosscall_core-x4
  — "Transfer your contacts, photos, videos, sms messages and
  call logs from Crosscall Core-X4 to an online storage and back
  to keep them safe and up to date." A cloud sync service, not a
  phone-to-USB guide — and the confirmation that the
  third-party route for Crosscall is an online storage account,
  not a USB drive); Photos to USB: OTG Backup (Play listing,
  com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the one-tap app alternative:
  copies the whole gallery to a pen drive in one tap, copies
  only new photos next time, warns about the FAT32 4 GB limit.
  Free with ads, one-time purchase to remove them. Generic
  Android, not a Crosscall guide, and it does not promise
  oldest-first order, detailed progress with Pause and Stop, or
  size verification of every file); cloud routes — Google Photos
  syncs the gallery to Google's servers, Google Drive backs
  data up to Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and Google backup
  restores data to a new phone from Google's cloud, not to a
  drive you hold; Google Photos' "Storage saver" setting
  compresses photos, and a cloud backup stops working the day
  you stop paying for storage. Neither route gives a folder of
  plain photo files on a USB drive.
  Crosscall specifics: OTG is confirmed on the Core-X5 (spec
  tables list USB On-The-Go support as Yes), on the Action-X5
  (Crosscall's own product sheet lists OTG as Yes), and on the
  Core-Z5 (spec tables list USB On-The-Go support as Yes) — but
  the Stellar-X5 spec pages do not list OTG either way, so the
  article tells owners of that model to check Settings >
  Storage before relying on it instead of claiming OTG across
  the range; all four phones have USB-C, so a USB-C flash drive
  plugs straight in and a regular USB-A drive needs a USB-C OTG
  adapter; the Core-X5 takes a microSD card up to 512GB, the
  Action-X5 takes one up to 512GB, and the Core-Z5 takes one up
  to 1TB, but a card is still not a USB drive: it stays in the
  phone and opening it anywhere else needs a card reader.
  Correction to the brief: the brief listed the Core-X5 /
  Action-X5 line as the scope, but the current line is wider —
  the Core-Z5 (OTG Yes, Android 12) and the Stellar-X5 (Android
  13, OTG unlisted) are current Crosscall phones too, so the
  article covers all four; the older Trekker-M1 phones are not
  Android 7.0+ smartphones — the Trekker-M1 and the Trekker-M1
  Core run Android 5.1, so SnapDisk cannot install on them at
  all.
  Crosscall's own tools: devicesfaq's backup advice (a computer
  copy with a USB cable and a file browser, or Google Drive
  with 15 GB free and Google Photos in the cloud) points at a
  computer or the cloud, never at a USB drive; the Trekker-X4
  manual's transfer advice (connect to a computer with a USB
  cable, Transfer files via MTP or Transfer photos via PTP)
  needs a computer; Google Photos (the stock photo backup on
  Crosscall Android phones) backs photos up to Google's cloud;
  Google Drive (Android's default built-in service, 15 GB free)
  backs data up to Google's cloud; a microSD card (up to 512GB
  on the Core-X5, up to 512GB on the Action-X5, up to 1TB on
  the Core-Z5) is a real local copy, but the card stays in the
  phone and needs a reader elsewhere; a computer copy (connect
  the phone to a computer using the USB cable and make a copy
  of the files using the file browser) lands on the PC, not on
  a USB drive; no Crosscall backup page points at a USB drive
  at all — none of them writes plain photo files to a USB drive
  automatically.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 18, 2026 (WL-82, 51st how-to):
  docs/crosscall-photos-to-usb.html. Query 50 scored in
  tool/growth/searches.md. Blog index (JSON-LD + post block),
  sitemap.xml, llms.txt, llms-full.txt, feed.xml (51 items)
  updated. PR #132 (article), merge dfb8350, CI run
  44304766625 (Android PR, success); live fetch after Pages
  deploy: article 200 (38,542 bytes, title "Back up Crosscall
  photos to a USB drive — SnapDisk"), feed.xml 51 items
  including Crosscall, sitemap 200; this row PR #133.
  Fallback for WL-83: Hammer (MyPhone Hammer line) — no Hammer
  article exists in docs/ yet.

  confirmation that a USB-C port does not guarantee host
  support); Photos to USB: OTG Backup (Play listing,

  data up to Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and Google backup
  restores data to a new phone from Google's cloud, not to a

  DuraSport take one up to 1TB, and the PRO 3 takes one up to
  2TB, but a card is still not a USB drive: it stays in the
  phone and opening it anywhere else needs a card reader.
  Correction to the brief: the brief listed the DuraForce line
  as the scope, but the older DuraForce phones are not Android
  7.0+ smartphones with USB host support — the original
  DuraForce runs Android 4.4.2 and the DuraForce XD runs
  Android 5.1, so SnapDisk cannot install on them at all, and
  the original DuraForce PRO's spec tables list USB OTG as No,
  so it cannot host a USB drive either; the DuraXV flip phones
  run a flip-phone build of Android and are a different shape
  of phone. The article is scoped to the Android DuraForce
  smartphones (Pro 2, Ultra 5G, PRO 3, and DuraSport).
  Kyocera's own tools: devicesfaq's backup advice (a computer
  copy with a USB cable and a file browser, or Google Drive
  with 15 GB free and Google Photos in the cloud) points at a
  computer or the cloud, never at a USB drive; Google Photos
  (the stock photo backup on Kyocera Android phones) backs
  photos up to Google's cloud; Google Drive (Android's default
  built-in service, 15 GB free) backs data up to Google's
  cloud; a microSD card (up to 512GB on the Pro 2, up to 1TB
  on the Ultra 5G and the DuraSport, up to 2TB on the PRO 3)
  is a real local copy, but the card stays in the phone and
  needs a reader elsewhere; a computer copy (connect the phone
  to a computer using the USB cable and make a copy of the
  files using the file browser) lands on the PC, not on a USB
  drive; no Kyocera backup page points at a USB drive at all —
  none of them writes plain photo files to a USB drive
  automatically.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Fallback for WL-82: Crosscall (Core-X5 / Action-X5 line)
  — no Crosscall article exists in docs/ yet.

  — a memory card reader and USB flash drive combination for

  — a portable SSD or flash drive with OTG support, a USB

  (sekin.in/how-to-transfer-files-from-android-phone-to-usb-drive-full-guide/

  one-tap app alternative: copies the whole gallery to a pen
  drive in one tap, copies only new photos next time, warns
  about the FAT32 4 GB limit. Free with ads, one-time
  purchase to remove them. Generic Android, not a Sonim
  guide, and it does not promise oldest-first order,
  detailed progress with Pause and Stop, or size
  verification of every file); cloud routes — Google Photos
  syncs the gallery to Google's servers, Google Drive backs
  data up to Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and Google
  backup restores data to a new phone from Google's cloud,
  not to a drive you hold; Google Photos' "Storage saver"
  setting compresses photos, and a cloud backup stops
  working the day you stop paying for storage. Neither route
  gives a folder of plain photo files on a USB drive.
  Sonim specifics: OTG is confirmed on the XP8 (user guide
  lists the port as "High Speed, OTG") — but the XP10
  datasheet does not list OTG either way, so the article
  tells owners of that model to check Settings > Storage
  before relying on it instead of claiming OTG across the
  range; one third-party spec page claims the XP10 supports
  USB OTG, but Sonim's own datasheet does not say so; both
  phones have USB-C, so a USB-C flash drive plugs straight
  in and a regular USB-A drive needs a USB-C OTG adapter;
  the XP8 takes a microSD card up to 128GB per Sonim's
  datasheet and the XP10 takes one up to 1TB, but a card is
  still not a USB drive: it stays in the phone and opening
  it anywhere else needs a card reader. Correction to the
  brief: the XP5s was listed as an Android model in scope,
  but Sonim's own datasheet lists its operating system as
  Proprietary OS — it is a feature phone with a 2.64-inch
  screen, not an Android phone, so SnapDisk cannot install
  on it at all and the article is scoped to the Android
  Sonim phones (XP8 and XP10); the XP3 flip phones run a
  proprietary OS too.
  Sonim's own tools: Sonim SCOUT (the preloaded
  device-management app) manages the phone but does not back
  up photos; Google Photos (the stock photo backup on Sonim
  Android phones) backs photos up to Google's cloud; Google
  Drive (Android's default built-in service, 15 GB free)
  backs data up to Google's cloud; a microSD card (up to
  128GB on the XP8, up to 1TB on the XP10) is a real local
  copy, but the card stays in the phone and needs a reader
  elsewhere; a computer copy (connect the phone to a
  computer using the USB cable and make a copy of the files
  using the file browser) lands on the PC, not on a USB
  drive; no Sonim backup page points at a USB drive at all
  — none of them writes plain photo files to a USB drive
  automatically. The XP5s runs a proprietary OS, not
  Android, so SnapDisk cannot install on it at all — the
  article is scoped to the Android Sonim phones (XP8 and
  XP10); the XP3 flip phones run a proprietary OS too.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Fallback for WL-81: Kyocera (DuraForce line) — no Kyocera
  article exists in docs/ yet.

  no backup advice at all, and the rest are generic manual
  guides. Frequently asked questions IIIF150 B3C
  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/iiif150-b3c —
  "How to backup my IIIF150 B3C?" The most economical method
  is to connect the phone to a computer using the USB cable and
  make a copy of the files using the file browser; a cloud
  backup is Google Drive with 15 GB free; for photos only,
  Google Photos; and "Pass media files to memory card." A
  per-model FAQ page, not a guide — and the confirmation that
  the IIIF150 backup advice is a computer copy or a cloud sync,
  not a USB drive); Frequently asked questions IIIF150 Air3
  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/iiif150-air3_eea
  — the same backup advice: a computer copy via USB cable and
  file browser, Google Drive, or Google Photos, plus passing
  media files to a memory card. A per-model FAQ page, not a
  guide); Frequently asked questions IIIF150 Air3S
  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/iiif150-air3s
  — the same backup advice. A per-model FAQ page, not a
  guide); Frequently asked questions IIIF150 Raptor LTD
  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/iiif150-raptor-ltd
  — the same backup advice. A per-model FAQ page, not a
  guide); Frequently asked questions IIIF150 B1 Pro
  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/iiif150-b1-pro
  — the same backup advice. A per-model FAQ page, not a
  guide); IIIF150 B3C Ultra-Thin Rugged Smartphone User Manual
  (manuals.plus/ae/1005008797996459 — the phone runs Android
  14 with a 50MP main camera and a 10800mAh battery, the spec
  table lists OTG as Support, the manual says to connect
  compatible USB devices such as flash drives via an OTG
  adapter to the Type-C port, and the TF card slot takes a
  card up to 1TB on a shared Nano+Nano/TF tray. A user manual,
  not a guide — and the confirmation that the B3C has OTG,
  with no backup advice in it at all); Techdows, "USB OTG:
  everything you need to know"
  (techdows.com/usb-otg-everything-you-need-to-know.html — an
  OTG explainer: plug a flash drive into the phone with an OTG
  adapter, open Files by Google, and copy the files. Generic
  Android, and a manual file-manager guide); YouTube, "How to
  Connect USB Drive to Android Phone to Move Photos & Videos
  (Best Method)" (youtube.com/watch?v=PYcbV25CczI — OTG
  connection and the phone's built-in file manager, no computer
  or internet needed. Generic Android, and a manual
  file-manager guide); Dr.Fone, "4 Practical Ways to Transfer
  Photos from Android to USB Flash Drive"
  (drfone.wondershare.com/android-transfer/how-to-transfer-photos-from-android-to-usb.html
  — transfer photos with an OTG adapter, through Google Drive,
  by drag-and-drop through a PC, or wirelessly through
  AirDroid. Generic Android, and the direct route is a manual
  file-manager copy); AnExplorer, "How to Transfer Files from
  Android to USB Drive (OTG, No Root)"
  (anexplorer.io/transfer/phone-to-usb — connect the drive
  with an OTG adapter, open AnExplorer, and copy the files
  with a progress bar, with a FAT32 4 GB limit note. Generic
  Android, and a manual file-manager guide — the closest
  generic guide to this article's job, but it still needs you
  to pick the files yourself); YouTube, "How to Transfer
  Photos, Videos and any Files from Android Phone to USB
  Pendrive (USB Drive Tutorial)"
  (youtube.com/watch?v=ch1evyu5PPM — OTG adapter and the
  phone's built-in file manager, with a FAT32 or exFAT drive.
  Generic Android, and a manual file-manager guide);
  sekin.in, "How To Transfer Files From Android Phone To USB
  Drive"
  (sekin.in/how-to-transfer-files-from-android-phone-to-usb-drive-full-guide/
  — connect a USB OTG drive or a regular flash drive through a
  USB-OTG adapter and copy the files with Files by Google, or
  through a Windows PC instead. Generic Android, and the direct
  route is a manual file-manager copy); Photos to USB: OTG
  Backup (Play listing, com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the one-tap
  app alternative: copies the whole gallery to a pen drive in
  one tap, copies only new photos next time, warns about the
  FAT32 4 GB limit. Free with ads, one-time purchase to remove
  them. Generic Android, not a IIIF150 guide, and it does not
  promise oldest-first order, detailed progress with Pause and
  Stop, or size verification of every file); cloud routes —
  Google Photos syncs the gallery to Google's servers, Google
  Drive backs data up to Google's cloud with 15 GB free, and
  Google backup restores data to a new phone from Google's
  cloud, not to a drive you hold; Google Photos' "Storage
  saver" setting compresses photos, and a cloud backup stops
  working the day you stop paying for storage. Neither route
  gives a folder of plain photo files on a USB drive.
  IIIF150 specifics: OTG is across the current IIIF150 B, Air,
  and Raptor lines: the B3C spec table lists OTG as Support,
  the B3C manual says to connect flash drives via an OTG
  adapter to the Type-C port, and the Air3, the B2 Ultra, the
  Air1 Ultra, the Air2 Ultra, and the Raptor all list USB
  On-The-Go as Yes; IIIF150 has no phone-to-USB backup tool of
  its own — the per-model FAQ pages' backup advice is only a
  computer copy, Google Drive, or Google Photos, and the stock
  photo backup is Google Photos; there is no IIIF150 backup
  page pointing at a USB drive at all.
  The B3C takes a TF card up to 1 TB, the Air3 takes one up to
  1 TB, and the B2 Ultra takes one up to 1 TB, but on a shared
  SIM slot — so a card costs a second SIM, unlike the dedicated
  slot on some other rugged phones — but a card is still not a
  USB drive: it stays in the phone and needs a reader
  elsewhere.
  IIIF150's own tools: a computer copy (the FAQ pages' most
  economical method, connect the phone to a computer using the
  USB cable and make a copy of the files using the file
  browser) lands on the PC, not on a USB drive; Google Drive
  (the FAQ pages' "default built-in service", 15 GB free)
  backs data up to Google's cloud; Google Photos (the stock
  photo backup, "If what you want is to make a backup only of
  the photos and videos made with your IIIF150 you can use
  Google Photos") backs photos up to Google's cloud; a TF card
  (the FAQ pages' "Pass media files to memory card", shared
  SIM slot, up to 1 TB on the B3C, the Air3, and the B2 Ultra)
  is a real local copy, but the card stays in the phone and
  needs a reader elsewhere; the B3C user manual gives no
  backup advice at all — none of them writes plain photo files
  to a USB drive automatically.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.

  — copy local originals to compatible USB storage with a

  — use an OTG adapter to plug in a USB drive, and it shows

  (smartprix.com/mobiles/cubot-kingkong-x-ppd1skjqy2ia — USB
  "Yes, USB-C", USB features "USB Tethering, USB on-the-go,

  triple rear camera, 10,200 mAh battery. A spec listing, not

  (gsmarena.com.bd/cubot-kingkong-mini-4/ — "USB Type-C 2.0",
  "OTG Yes", microSDXC on a shared SIM slot. A spec listing,
  not a guide — and the confirmation that the KingKong Mini 4
  has OTG); Cubot KingKong Dura specs
  (m.gsmarena.com/cubot_kingkong_dura-ampp-14854.php — "USB
  Type-C 2.0, OTG", announced July 2026, microSDXC on a
  shared SIM slot. A spec listing, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the KingKong Dura has OTG); Cubot
  KingKong X Pro specs
  (smartprix.com/mobiles/cubot-kingkong-x-pro-ppd1yrc091hh —
  USB "Yes, USB-C", USB features "USB Tethering, USB
  on-the-go, USB Charging, USB Storage", Android 14. A spec
  listing, not a guide — and the confirmation that the
  KingKong X Pro has OTG); Google backup on Cubot Note 50
  (tutorial.info/video/how-to-perform-a-google-backup-on-cubot-note-50
  — Settings, System, Backup, link a Google account, and back
  up to Google One, with 15 GB free on the Google account. A
  Cubot-specific backup guide — and the confirmation that
  Cubot's own backup route is Google's cloud, not a USB
  drive); How to backup Cubot X11
  (mobilespecs.net/phone/backup/Cubot/CUBOT_X11.html — sync
  personal data with Google cloud storage, use Google Photos
  for photos, and the other methods are TWRP Recovery, CWM
  Recovery, and Titanium Backup. A Cubot-specific backup page
  — and the confirmation that the non-cloud methods need a
  custom recovery or root); How to backup Cubot KingKong
  MINI 3
  (mobilespecs.net/phone/backup/Cubot/Cubot_King_Kong_MINI_3.html
  — the same advice for the KingKong Mini 3: Google cloud
  sync, Google Photos for photos, then TWRP, CWM, and
  Titanium Backup. A Cubot-specific backup page — and the
  confirmation that the KingKong line's own backup advice is
  cloud or root-only tools); Cubot P40 user manual
  (cdn4.guidesimo.com/view/2100/2100082-p40.pdf — "Backup
  Data – Will back up all application data, WLAN passwords,
  and other settings to Google's servers", and to transfer
  files, select MTP in the connection mode. A manual, not a
  guide — and the confirmation that Cubot's own backup is to
  Google's servers and its file transfer is a PC copy over
  MTP); cloud routes — Google Photos syncs the gallery to
  Google's servers, Google backup restores data to a new
  phone from Google's cloud, not to a drive you hold; Google
  Photos' "Storage saver" setting compresses photos, and a
  cloud backup stops working the day you stop paying for
  storage. Neither route gives a folder of plain photo files
  on a USB drive.
  Cubot specifics: OTG is across the current Cubot KingKong
  family: spec tables list USB On-The-Go as Yes for the
  KingKong X, the KingKong 11, and the KingKong Mini 4, the
  KingKong Dura lists USB Type-C 2.0 with OTG, and the
  KingKong X Pro lists USB on-the-go too; Cubot has no
  phone-to-USB backup tool of its own — the per-model backup
  guides all point to Google cloud sync, Google Photos, or
  TWRP, CWM, and Titanium Backup, which need a custom
  recovery or root, and the stock photo backup is Google
  Photos; there is no Cubot backup FAQ on devicesfaq at all.
  The KingKong X takes a microSD card up to 1 TB and the
  KingKong 11 takes one too, but on a shared SIM slot — so a
  card costs a second SIM, unlike the dedicated slot on some
  other rugged phones — but a card is still not a USB drive:
  it stays in the phone and needs a reader elsewhere.
  Cubot's own tools: Google backup (Settings > System >
  Backup, "Backup by Google One" on, to Google's cloud, 15 GB
  free) backs the phone up to Google's cloud; Google Photos
  (Photos settings > Backup on) backs photos up to Google's
  cloud; the P40 manual's backup mention is to Google's
  servers and its file transfer is MTP to a PC; a microSD
  card (shared SIM slot, up to 1 TB on the KingKong X) is a
  real local copy, but the card stays in the phone and needs
  a reader elsewhere; TWRP, CWM, and Titanium Backup need a
  custom recovery or root — none of them writes plain photo
  files to a USB drive automatically.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.

  (BackupEngine.copyWithResume — no decode/re-encode) with size
  verification after copy. Search evidence (Nov 17, 2026 live
  web search): there is no CAT-specific phone-to-USB guide in
  the top results at all — the CAT-specific results are
  per-model FAQ pages whose backup advice is a computer copy
  or a cloud backup, and the rest are generic manual guides.
  devicesfaq, "Frequently asked questions Cat S75"
  (devicesfaq.com/en/frequently-asked-questions/cat-s75 —
  "How to backup my Cat S75?" The most economical method is
  to connect the Cat S75 to a computer using the USB cable
  and make a copy of the files using the file browser; for a
  cloud backup use Google Drive (15 GB free); for photos and
  videos only, use Google Photos, configured to upload
  automatically or when on Wi-Fi. CAT-specific, but
  PC-or-cloud. No phone-to-USB route in it at all);
  VaultDrive: Back Up Phone Photos to a USB Drive, No
  Computer (coticsy.com/vaultdrive.html — a phone-to-USB
  backup app page: plug a flash drive or SSD into your phone,
  choose what to back up and tap Start, copies original
  photos, videos and files straight to the drive. Free to try,
  Pro $19.99 once, no account, Android 8 or later. Generic,
  not a CAT guide); AskFilo, "how to transfer pictures from
  an android phone to a flashdrive"
  (askfilo.com/user-question-answers-smart-solutions/how-to-transfer-pictures-from-an-android-phone-to-a-3436303837303432
  — USB OTG adapter plus a file manager: find the DCIM
  folder, open Camera, select the photos, copy to the USB
  drive — or use a PC or Mac as a middleman. Generic Android,
  and the direct route is manual select-copy-paste); YouTube,
  "How to Connect USB Drive to Android Phone to Move Photos &
  Videos (Best Method)" (youtube.com/watch?v=PYcbV25CczI —
  OTG connection and the phone's built-in file manager, no
  computer or internet needed. Generic Android, and a manual
  file-manager guide); Photos to USB: OTG Backup (Play
  listing, com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb — the one-tap app
  alternative; it did surface in this query's top results:
  copies the whole gallery to a pen drive in one tap, copies
  only new photos next time, warns about the FAT32 4 GB
  limit. Free with ads, one-time purchase to remove them.
  Generic Android, not a CAT guide, and it does not promise
  oldest-first order, detailed progress with Pause and Stop,
  or size verification of every file); Alibaba, "How To
  Quickly Backup Your Phone Photos Without Using Cloud
  Storage"
  (alibaba.com/product-insights/how-to-quickly-backup-your-phone-photos-without-using-cloud-storage.html
  — USB cable + computer, portable SSD/flash drive with OTG +
  file manager, NAS, Wi-Fi transfer apps. Generic Android,
  and the direct route is manual select-copy-paste with the
  file manager); Google Photos Help, "Back up photos &
  videos" (support.google.com/photos/answer/6193313 —
  automatically save your photos and videos to your Google
  Account when you turn on backup. A cloud backup to Google's
  servers — not a phone-to-USB copy); Google Android Help,
  "Back up your phone"
  (support.google.com/android/answer/12760783 — Settings >
  Google > All services > Backup, turn on Backup by Google
  One, tap Back up now. A cloud backup to Google's servers —
  not a phone-to-USB copy); Cat S62 Pro vs Cat S75 specs
  (smartprix.com/mobiles/cat_s62_pro_vs_cat_s75-cpd1xabx2rup_pd1kvjohf8x.php
  — USB "Yes, USB-C v2.0", USB features "USB on-the-go, USB
  Charging" for both models. A spec comparison, not a guide —
  and the confirmation that the S75 and the S62 Pro have
  OTG); Cat S53 specs (m.gsmarena.com/cat_s53-ampp-11766.php
  — "USB Type-C 3.0, OTG", microSDXC card slot using the
  shared SIM slot, 128 GB of storage, 6 GB RAM, 5,500 mAh
  battery. A spec listing, not a guide — and the
  confirmation that the S53 has OTG); Notebookcheck, "The
  CAT S62 Pro Rugged Smartphone Review"
  (notebookcheck.net/The-CAT-S62-Pro-Rugged-Smartphone-Review-Robustness-burdened-by-big-compromises.496845.0.html
  — "Thanks to USB OTG functionality, users can connect
  peripheral devices to the CAT smartphone." A review, not a
  guide — and a second confirmation that the S62 Pro has
  OTG); cloud routes — Google Photos syncs the gallery to
  Google's servers, Google backup restores data to a new
  phone from Google's cloud, not to a drive you hold; Google
  Photos' "Storage saver" setting compresses photos, and a
  cloud backup stops working the day you stop paying for
  storage. Neither route gives a folder of plain photo files
  on a USB drive.
  CAT specifics: OTG is across the current CAT rugged range:
  spec tables list USB Type-C 2.0 with OTG for the S75 and
  the S62 Pro, and USB Type-C 3.0 with OTG for the S53; CAT
  has no phone-to-USB backup tool of its own — the
  per-model backup guides all point to a computer copy,
  Google Drive, or Google Photos, with a microSD card as the
  local option; the S75 and the S53 both take a microSDXC
  card that uses the shared SIM slot (up to 512 GB on the
  S53), while the S62 Pro has a dedicated microSDXC slot,
  while the stock photo backup is Google Photos.
  CAT's own tools: Google backup (Settings > Google > All
  services > Backup, "Backup by Google One" on, to Google's
  cloud, 15 GB free) backs the phone up to Google's cloud;
  Google Photos (Photos settings > Backup on) backs photos up
  to Google's cloud; the per-model guides' most economical
  method (USB cable, copy the files with the file browser)
  backs the phone up to the PC; a microSD card (expandable
  with microSD, uses the second SIM slot) is a real local
  copy, but the card stays in the phone and needs a reader
  elsewhere — none of them writes plain photo files to a USB
  drive automatically.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.
  Article published Nov 17, 2026 (WL-72, 41st how-to):
  docs/cat-photos-to-usb.html. Query 40 scored in
  tool/growth/searches.md. Blog index (JSON-LD + post block),

  Blackview's own backup guide: 1. Google Account Backup, 2.

  Titanium Backup with root), 4. Backup to External Storage
  ("many Android phones support the use of a microSD card or

  for a local backup"), 5. ADB backup (needs a computer and
  command-line tools), 6. Custom recovery and full system

  check it appears as external storage, navigate through

## WL-64 — Emulator end-to-end proof: full backup flow green (DONE)

- Owner: Product Lead agent. State: DONE Nov 18, 2026.
  Per Den's Nov 17 rule (product-framework commit 8803ffc):
  the emulator end-to-end pass is the proof; a real device is
  never required and never a gate; never ask him to test on
  his phone. Full backup flow verified on the emulator with
  virtual external storage, every step by evidence, not claims:
  disk detection (StorageReceiver mount broadcast -> offer
  notification) -> offer backup -> copy oldest-first (3 seeded
  photos, byte-verified at the destination) -> progress display
  (notification with progress bar, Pause/Stop actions) -> Pause
  (copy freezes, resume continues byte-exact) -> Stop (finished
  files kept, in-flight .part deleted) -> DONE notification
  ("Backup complete"). Evidence: emulator smoke run
  44219739812, green in 27m20s, JUnit XML in the run's
  smoke-evidence artifact: 18 tests, 0 failures, 0 errors,
  0 skipped — FullFlowInstrumentedTest (mount->offer,
  serviceCopies oldest-first + progress + DONE, pause/resume
  via notification actions, stop via notification action) plus
  NotificationScreenshotTest (offer/progress/done) and the
  engine/billing suites. PR #95 (14 commits), merge 57bc3ab,
  merged 2026-11-18T02:25:54Z; PR CI green.
  Two real findings fixed along the way, both in PR #95: (1) a
  genuine app bug — BackupService.publish() posted the terminal
  DONE/ERROR/LIMIT notification while still foreground, so
  stopForeground() removed it and the user never saw "Backup
  complete" (smoke runs 44199483316, 44203932318, 44210493197);
  fixed by detaching first (stopForeground(false)) and then
  posting via manager.notify(). (2) Two test-harness timing
  lessons, test-only: the DONE title must be asserted promptly
  at DONE time — the harness destroys MainActivity and removes
  both tasks ~15 s after DONE, which cancels the notification,
  so a poll after the ~17 s file verification always saw
  active=[] (run 44210493197's logcat proved the service DID
  post it: its sound played); and the seed DATE_TAKEN converge
  poll is now a warning, not a failure — the MediaStore scanner
  restores DATE_TAKEN from EXIF asynchronously, and with
  taken=0 the effective date (DATE_MODIFIED) still sorts
  sequentially seeded files oldest-first; the enumeration-order
  assert is the real gate.
  "Real-device proof (Pixel 9a + real USB disk)" removed from
  the Blocked (owner) lists in the v0.1.2 and v0.1.1 entries
  below (this row's PR). Honest limit: the emulator has no USB
  mount event, so the mount trigger itself is exercised via a
  synthetic broadcast, not a real OTG plug; the real-disk check
  (NTFS read-only detection) stays open in the Open section,
  route: Firebase Test Lab free tier, never his phone.

  own and Google Photos is the cloud backup behind it);
  Redmi's own backup routes are Xiaomi Cloud (gallery sync
  and backup to a Xiaomi account, free storage limited) and
  Google's (Google Photos, Google backup via Google One);
  OTG: spec tables list USB Type-C 2.0 with OTG for the Note
  14 Pro 4G and the Note 14 Pro 5G; the microSD slot varies
  by model — the Note 14 Pro 4G has a microSDXC slot (uses
  the shared SIM slot) while the Note 14 Pro 5G has none —
  check your own model's specs; on many HyperOS phones OTG
  is off by default — enable it in Settings > Additional
  settings > OTG (on some phones under More settings > OTG),
  and the switch turns itself off after about 10 minutes
  without a drive (same switch as the Xiaomi guide, WL-era,
  verified there); all Redmi phones are USB-C. Redmi's own
  tools: Xiaomi Cloud (Settings > Xiaomi account > Xiaomi
  Cloud, gallery sync and backup to Xiaomi's servers, free
  storage limited) and Google backup (Settings > Google >
  Backup, Back up by Google One) restore to a new phone from
  the cloud; Google Photos (Photos settings > Backup, to
  Google's cloud, 15 GB free) backs photos up to Google's
  cloud; the SD-card route copies to a card, not a drive, and
  only some Redmi models have a slot (Note 14 Pro 4G does,
  Note 14 Pro 5G does not); Google Photos / Google One /
  Xiaomi Cloud back photos up to their clouds — none of them
  writes plain photo files to a USB drive.
- Verdict: how-to article material, not a listing keyword.

  drive, and only the M series has a slot; Google Photos /
  Google One / Xiaomi Cloud back photos up to their clouds —
  none of them writes plain photo files to a USB drive.

  easier-with-free-2tb-cloud-storage/ — eligible Tecno
  phones launched from December 2025 get three months of
  Google AI Plus with 2TB of cloud storage; a cloud offer,
  not a USB copy). Tecno specifics: Tecno phones run HiOS
  (Tecno's Android skin); stock file manager is File Manager
  (Transsion File Manager, com.transsion.filemanagerx);
  no confirmed stock gallery name was found, so the article
  does not name one; an OTG toggle location is reported in
  the connection settings on the Pova 4, but the source is
  a low-quality guide — treat it as a lead, not a fact;
  if nothing appears, check Settings > Storage; OTG varies
  by model and regional variant (POP 7 Pro, CAMON 20 Pro,
  POVA 7, SPARK Go 2024, Pova 5 Pro listed; Pop 3 lists OTG
  in its specs but owners report it not enabled); modern
  Tecno phones are USB-C, older ones Micro-USB; whether a
  model has a microSD slot varies by model — check the
  model's specs. Tecno's own tools: built-in Backup &
  Restore (Settings > System > Back up & reset, Local backup
  or Phone backup, to internal storage or an SD card);
  TECNO Cloud / HiOS Cloud (Settings > TECNO Cloud, TECNO
  account) backs photos up to Tecno's cloud; Google backup
  (Settings > Google > Backup, Back up by Google One)
  restores to a new phone from the cloud; Coolmuster backs
  the phone up to a PC; the SD-card route copies to a card,
  not a drive; Google Photos / Google One back photos up to
  Google's cloud (15 GB free) — none of them writes plain
  photo files to a USB drive.

  Samsung, Google, Motorola — a stick to buy, not a guide;
  its URL was truncated in the search output, so the article
  names it without linking); AnExplorer guide (manual
  select-copy-paste, anexplorer.io/transfer/phone-to-usb; the
  only one showing a progress bar for the manual copy, still no

  manager manual copy, suggests Move), Dr.Fone (Google Drive /
  PC drag-and-drop / OTG manual copy / AirDroid); Photos to USB:
  OTG Backup (Play listing, com.arzdrz.photos_to_usb) is the
  one non-manual result — a real phone-to-USB app, one-tap
  whole-gallery copy with resume, free with ads — generic
  Android, not an LG guide, and it does not promise
  oldest-first order, detailed progress with Pause and Stop, or
  size verification, so the article says so honestly instead of
  claiming SnapDisk is the only one-tap option. Cloud is Google
  Photos — cloud, not USB. LG specifics stated honestly: LG
  left the phone business in April 2021 (wound down July 31,
  2021) — no new LG phones, no new LG tools, and LG Mobile
  Switch is no longer updated (struggles on newer Android
  versions); stock file manager is LG File Manager (Management
  > File Manager), stock gallery is Gallery; no OTG toggle —
  plug the drive in and it mounts; late LG phones (G7, G8, V30
  and later, Velvet, Wing, V60) are USB-C, older ones
  Micro-USB; most LG phones have a microSD slot. LG's own
  tools: LG Mobile Switch is phone-to-phone migration (USB
  cable / Wi-Fi / SD card), LG Bridge is PC software that backs
  the phone up to a computer, LG Backup (Settings, Backup &
  reset) writes a backup to an SD card or the cloud — none of
  them writes plain photo files to a USB drive. Honest contrast
  stated in the TL;DR: LG File Manager works but is manual
  select-copy-paste with no progress, no resume, no

  a PC, or a backup package — not plain photos on a USB drive;

## WL-34 — Answer the Android 15 six-hour dataSync timeout before first release (DONE)

- Owner: Implementation lead. Due: Nov 19. State: DONE.
  Evidence: PR #30 (commit 12fe757, merge 81ed0e4); CI run
  43791513967 green (build pass, 1m47s).
  `BackupService.onTimeout` pauses with the same clean paused state
  as a disk detach (PAUSED + full error, `.part` kept, finished
  files kept), plain notification, then `stopSelf()`; `CopyControl.timedOut`
  unwinds the engine to `CopyResult.Paused` at the next buffer
  boundary; resume reuses the detach path (open the app first to
  reset the timer). A refused `startForeground` after the budget is
  exhausted surfaces as a full ERROR state, never a crash. Two unit
  tests prove the timeout path pauses cleanly and resumes byte-exact.
  Target-SDK note added to the implementation spec §2 (workspace spec
  mirror `docs/implementation-spec.md` — the repo carries no spec .md;
  the Notion hub page carries the same paragraph — verified present
  Nov 16, see the follow-up entry below). Timeout
  behavior verified against Android Developers "Foreground service
  timeouts". Real-device check closed by the Nov 17 emulator-proof
  rule (WL-64): the emulator end-to-end pass is the proof; never
  his phone.

  email list but has no per-post canonical field and weaker how-to
  discovery. Canonical URLs stay the GitHub Pages ones either way.
  Owner step: create a Medium publication for SnapDisk and grant
  access — then cross-posting of the 5 articles can start.

## 2026-11-14 — v0.1.1 MVP shipped (v0.1.0 deleted, unannounced)

- Owner: Implementation + Publish lead. State: DONE (GitHub Release
  v0.1.1, PR + Release green; emulator smoke run 43621462440 —
  verdict below).
  Evidence: repo `dzianisv/photo-backup`, tag `v0.1.1`,
  APK https://github.com/dzianisv/photo-backup/releases/download/v0.1.1/app-release.apk
  (11,535,003 bytes; aapt2: dev.dzianisv.snapdisk, versionCode 2,
  versionName 0.1.1, minSdk 24, targetSdk 36; apksigner verify OK).
  CI: PR 43621458782 success, Release 43621462565 success,
  Smoke 43621462440 FAILED — attempt 2 (rerun) ran all 10
  instrumented tests: 8 passed, 2 failed (pauseResumeAndStop +
  settingsScreenShowsDefaultFoldersStartAndPro, same as v0.1.0;
  JUnit XML in the run's smoke-evidence artifact). The earlier
  "both attempts failed on the boot flake, no test ran" note was
  wrong for attempt 2; attempt 1's failure mode was not verified.
  No green tag smoke for v0.1.1: it is released but NOT
  smoke-verified; v0.1.2 is the first smoke-verified release.
  The 2 real failures (found by v0.1.0 smoke run 43618997791):
  (1) pause race — the engine wrote one 4 KB chunk read while
  paused (expected 28672, got 32768); (2) MainActivity fired two
  permission launchers back-to-back, the second was dropped, the
  notifications dialog blocked the settings screen (`No compose
  hierarchies found`). Both fixed in PR #2 (merge 1324c75); v0.1.2
  (branch smoke 43622646593 10/10 + tag smoke 43624496788 10/10)
  is green.
  v0.1.0 was tagged, then DELETED (never announced): its release run
  43618997754 failed lintVitalRelease (registerForActivityResult
  needs Fragment >= 1.3.0 — fixed by adding fragment-ktx 1.8.5),
  and its smoke run 43618997791 ran all 10 tests — 8 passed, 2
  failed on real assertions (pause race, expected 28672 got 32768;
  settings screen "No compose hierarchies found" after 154.5 s),
  not a boot flake. Build fixes on the way to green: kotlin-jvm/android plugin
  collision (compose plugin already registers the `kotlin`
  extension — compose plugin only, no kotlinOptions), 4 compile
  errors (themes.xml Material3 parent, `isPro` scope in
  BillingManager.apply, BillingManager Context not stored, missing
  ServiceInfo import), AppCompat `?attr/colorControlNormal` tint in
  notification icons (removed; paths already white), broken vector
  XML after the tint removal, SpeedWindow window-boundary prune,
  FilesystemInfo root-mount `/` never matching, racy pause test
  (in-flight chunk after the snapshot).
  Scope: settings screen (From/To SAF pickers, defaults
  /sdcard/DCIM + /disk/DCIM), mount detection (USB attach +
  MEDIA_MOUNTED), offer notification (Backup/Cancel), foreground
  service copy with progress notification (%, copied/total, rolling
  5s speed, ETA) + Pause/Stop, oldest-first (photos + videos,
  MediaStore DATE_TAKEN asc), skip-if-exists + `.part` resume +
  size verification, verify retry-once + skip-and-continue, FAT32
  >4 GB skip, disk-detach pause with full error + resume, different
  -disk refusal, full-error screen with Copy (incl. version +
  Android version), free 5 GB cap, Pro billing wired ($9.99
  `pro_unlock`, auto-backup on connect).
  Decisions recorded: SnapDisk name + $9.99 Pro (Product Lead,
  delegated by owner, 2026-11-14).
  Product-framework push: products/photo-backup.md pushed cleanly
  (commit 5712671, no force-push).
  Blocked (owner): Play Console account ($25 — the one remaining
  Play gate), stable upload keystore, PostHog project, Sentry DSN,
  sandbox Pro purchase (needs the Play Console app + product).
